Look! My child, who is coming unto you;
Look up, my little one, now your trouble
Goes away, away;
Look! Above you flies one who guards you.
Whose presence brings you joy.
Now your sorrow has departed.
Ah, you look!
See the eagles flying over you.
From up above they come,
From the clear blue sky where Father dwells;
They to you this peace-bringing solace give.

Light-hearted.

ix

Preface
Craniofacial and cleft surgery is a noble craft and a healing art. To understand the science of craniofacial
surgery is the beginning—the goal is to achieve aesthetic excellence, which is more art than science. I
believe that careful technique, experience, and a sense of aesthetic balance and harmony is important in
performing this three-dimensional surgery. This two volume atlas contains proven techniques, new and old,
that allow previsualization of the surgical steps in preparation for the performance of craniofacial and cleft
surgery.
This Atlas was written at the request of the many students, fellows, and surgeons from the United States and
abroad who have requested a better way to visualize and understand the surgical techniques used by the authors
and contributors. This Atlas is meant to augment knowledge and to demonstrate proven techniques for surgeons
in order to improve their methods and results. There are brief, pointed comments to help surgeons understand
the procedure; left out is the philosophy, thinking, selection, sequencing, and planning of the procedures. It is
meant to be helpful to all surgeons involved in or entering into craniofacial and/or cleft lip and palate surgery,
and to serve as a resource for those interested in improving or expanding their perspectives on surgery of the
cranial vault, head, and face. The text includes procedures of interest to plastic and reconstructive, maxillofacial,
and ophthalmic plastic surgeons, as well as otolaryngologists and pediatric neurosurgeons.
This Atlas will ultimately be judged on its own merits and usefulness by those surgeons interested in its
contents. It is intended to be a step-by-step manual used to learn new procedures or to prepare for tomorrow's
surgery by reviewing the surgical steps necessary for that procedure. Take it to the operating room, soil it with
coffee or your favorite prep solution, but use it. Use it not as another attractive atlas with beautiful color
illustrations and pictures to be placed in your library as a passive adornment, but as a user-friendly atlas.
This Atlas evolved from my close collaboration with Janusz Bardach through over 25 years of writing,
lecturing, giving courses together, operating together, and demonstrating surgery, as well as our cochairing a
symposium on surgical techniques in cleft lip and palate for 18 continuous years. He is truly an international
authority and true scholar and professor of cleft lip and palate surgery. It was through these courses and other
symposiums that I had an opportunity to meet the contributors to this Atlas. Hearing their presentations,
viewing their results, and holding many panels and discussions with them about their techniques and results
led to their involvement. They all have performed the procedures described, and have critically looked at and
shared their results, trying to improve their performance. The end results are the techniques and procedures
that have been very carefully outlined in their contributions.
The contributors are truly international, representing nine countries. Their common bond is their ability
to achieve excellence with their surgical skills. They have done more than this in sharing their experience
with their peers through the written or spoken word.
My international involvement in traveling and teaching craniofacial surgery has made me aware of the
desperate need in many parts of the world for the detailed surgical procedures presented in this Atlas. There
are many parts of the world where this surgery does not exist. I believe we have a humanitarian obligation
to help all patients with these deformities and to share the developments, refinements, and improvements that
have occurred as a result of the advances made in this field in the last few years. This Atlas presents these
techniques developed by proven authorities.
Craniofacial and cleft surgery requires a multidisciplinary approach of a dedicated team to deliver the
optimal care demonstrated in this Atlas. No longer can a surgeon work in isolation and achieve the excellent
results demonstrated in these two volumes. The support of a pediatric hospital with pediatric anesthesiologists and intensivists who are trained in the care of children who have had major intracranial procedures,
or of infants with major airway problems or other specialized pediatric problems is required.

xi

xii / PREFACE

My patients and I have been fortunate to have the availability of an excellent team that was assembled ten
years ago as a dedicated center in Dallas for the treatment of these deformities. All the techniques I have
presented are based on my personal experience gained over three decades of performing over 8000
craniofacial and cleft lip and palate procedures. This Atlas represents the culmination of some of the best
techniques that I use today in performing this work.
I have spent many years traveling to foreign countries and instructing local doctors in the surgical
treatment of craniofacial and cleft deformities. My hope is that this Atlas will be an extension of this work,
that surgeons throughout the world will be able to use the described techniques to help their patients live
normal, fulfilling lives.
Kenneth E. Salyer

Acknowledgments
Inspiration for this Atlas came from the love, motivation, and understanding that I have gained from children
with craniofacial deformities and their families. Special recognition and thanks go to Pulitzer-Prize-winning
photographer Eddie Adams, whose cover and chapter opening photographs in Volume I capture the
emotions and feelings of these families and this life-changing surgery. Credit for much of the vision and
motivation for excellence goes to my mentors, teachers, and the pioneer surgeons with whom I have had the
privilege to be associated, as well as to all my students, residents, and fellows.
Since coauthoring the Atlas of Craniomaxillofacial Surgery in 1982, I have had numerous inquiries and
requests to do an updated Atlas. This Atlas contains craniofacial surgery advances and updates and now
includes cleft lip and palate surgery.
Both volumes of the Atlas feature detailed instructional artwork that allows better understanding and
visualization of the surgical procedures. I am deeply grateful to both artists, William Winn and Hani Elkadi.
Although each artist's work is very different, the division of their work into two separate companion volumes
works well together to enhance understanding and learning of these techniques.
My special thanks go to William Winn, whom I have admired and worked with for nearly 30 years. His
ability to artistically render the important surgical and technical steps of craniofacial surgery into an
understandable visual image is a special art form unto itself. The performance of this surgery becomes more
realistic and possible through his beautiful illustrative art. Bill also did all the initial pencil sketches for my
contributions to Volume II.
Special recognition and thanks go to Dr. Janusz Bardach for his precise writing and editing, which blends
the two volumes as companion pieces. Over the last 25 years he has provided me with inspiration and
guidance in research, clinical care, and the ability to communicate those thoughts in writing.
Dr. Bardach worked closely with Hani Elkadi, whose artistic technique provides the detail necessary to
understand the complicated step-by-step nature of the cleft surgery in Volume II. Many of these techniques
have never been presented so clearly.
This Atlas represents the work of many other people. My thanks and appreciation go to each contributing
author for their time and effort in bringing their surgical techniques to life and making them useful.
The surgery that I perform, the techniques, and this Atlas would not be possible without the support of
our dedicated Dallas craniofacial team. I am particularly indebted to Edward Genecov, D.D.S, whose
orthodontic care and support has contributed so significantly to the care of these patients over 30 years.
I would like to thank my associates, Dr. Eric H. Hubli, for the use of his patient photographs in Figures
4-121 through 4-124, and Dr. Robert Taylor for the use of his patient photographs in Figures 4-204 through
4-206. Both cases present good results using the techniques demonstrated.
A very special thanks goes to Kimberly Hickey and Stacey Black, who organized the patient photographs
and worked with the layout and design; Janice Mauck for her continued dedicated effort and support in all
aspects of the project, including all transcribing; and to the media department at Medical City Dallas
Hospital for their reproductions and assistance, especially Mike Lorfing and Terry Cockerham. I am
particularly grateful for the assistance of Kathleen Gleeson, whose work on both volumes aided in the
copyediting and proofreading of the text.
The project was initiated with the insight and support of Lisa Berger at Raven Press. I want to
acknowledge the support and encouragement given to me by the publisher, particularly Kathey Alexander,
Danette Knopp, Anne Sydor, and Diana Andrews, all of whom gave of their time and special talents to make
this Atlas an expression of the work as I envisioned it. They represent the best with whom I have worked.

BASIC SURGICAL
••••••••••••••••••••••

TECHNIQUES

BASIC SURGICAL TECHNIQUES

Craniofacial surgery requires an experienced team,
which includes the craniofacial surgeon, neurosurgeon, anesthesiologist, and, depending on the case,
an otolaryngologist, maxillofacial surgeon, ophthalmologist, and orthodontist. In many cases, it is
important to have two teams working simultaneously—one in the area of the head and the other, for
example, at the patient’s side obtaining bone or cartilage implants or skin grafts. The craniofacial surgeon is the team leader, although during different
stages of the operation, the leading role may be assumed by the neurosurgeon or another specialist.
Major craniofacial surgery is performed under
controlled hypotension (goal of mean arterial pres-

3

sure, 50–55 mm Hg), normovolemic hemodilution
(Hct, 24–27%) prior to incision, and hyperventilation (paCO2, 22–28 mm Hg) for brain shrinkage.
Controlled hypotension is achieved by the use of
oral clonidine as part of the preoperative preparation with the addition of IV labetalol as needed.
Normovolemic hemodilution is performed after
the airway has been secured and all IV and arterial
lines are in place. Replacement fluids are 2–3 ml
RL: 1 ml whole blood for half the collection and a
1 : 1 ratio of 5% plasma protein fraction for the
other half of the collection. For patients heavier
than 50 kg, the entire collection may be replaced
with RL at 2–3 ml of whole blood collected.

4

CHAPTER 1

Figure 1-1 The basic approach to the craniofacial
skeleton, whether intracranially or extracranially, is
through the bicoronal skin incision. I use a zig-zag design because it creates a less visible scar on the hairy
portion of the skull. I do not shave the hair before
surgery. The hair is washed the night before, and a standard preparation is used on the face and head at the
time of surgery. Depending on the condition, the incision may be placed more anteriorly or more posteriorly.
An anteriorly placed incision allows easy undermining
and downward rotation of the scalp and forehead flap.
The front of the facial skeleton can be easily reached
through this incision. A posteriorly placed incision is
used only when a specific area in the posterior half of
the skull needs exposure.

Figure 1-2 A bicoronal incision is used to turn down
the scalp and pericranium, exposing the glabella, nasal
bones, and supraorbital rims. Note that the temporalis
muscle is turned down on the right side, a procedure I
do not normally use unless I need to gain access to the
area.
Figure 1-3 The supraorbital nerve is released from the
bony canal with an osteotome. A malleable retractor
protects the globe. Frequently the nerve is contained
within a notch and can be pulled downward without
using an osteotome.
Figure 1-4 The osteotome is used to create a notch in
the supraorbital rim in order to release the supraorbital
nerve.

Figure 1-5 The temporalis muscle is turned down using a periosteal elevator, Colorado needle, and a lighted
retractor, and dissected from the lateral orbital wall.
This maneuver is frequently necessary for procedures
such as the Lefort III, in which zygomatic access to the
pterygomaxillary region is required.
Figure 1-6 Dissection of the soft tissue over the malar
bone is performed with a periosteal elevator. The globe

is retracted, providing access to the anterior surface of
the facial skeleton.
Figure 1-7 Dissection over the malar bone and maxilla is extended with a periosteal dissector extending
from the lateral orbital wall along the zygomatic arch
without damaging the frontalis branch of the facial
nerve. This provides excellent access to the orbit, along
the inferior orbital rim, and to the floor of the orbit.

■ INTRACRANIAL EXPOSURE
Figure 1-8 The intracranial approach shown here is
used in an infant when the fontanelle is not completely
fused. By using a bicoronal incision, the skin flap is
turned down. The frontal view shows the supraorbital
nerves; the dotted line outlines the incisions in the pericranium.
Figure 1-9 By using a dissector, the pediatric neurosurgeon dissects the pericranium from the fontanelle

through an incision along the bony edge. The dura remains attached to prevent it from being damaged.
Figure 1-10 After the bifrontal craniotomy and removal of the bone, the left temporal lobe is retracted
near the middle lobe, while the dissection is continued
at the anterior bone keel. The dissection must be performed carefully to prevent tearing the dura.

CHAPTER 1

■ INTRAORAL EXPOSURE
Figure 1-11 Intraoral exposure can be achieved
through an incision in the gingivobuccal sulcus. This
incision exposes the anterior surface of the facial skeleton, including the maxilla, zygoma, and inferior orbital
rim. When combined with a bicoronal incision, access
to the entire facial skeleton is enhanced.
Figure 1-12 The incision is made in the gingivobuccal sulcus, leaving 1–2 mm on the labial side to allow
for easy closure of the mucosa incision.

Figure 1-13 When exposing the anterior surface of the
maxilla, special attention must be paid to preserve the
infraorbital nerve. Through the same incision, dissection in the subperiosteal plane may be carried over the
malar bone and zygomatic arch. This approach, along
with a bicoronal incision, facilitates the separation of
the soft tissue over the malar region and inferior and
lateral orbital walls. Retractors must be placed on the
floor of the orbit to protect the globe if Lefort or lamellar-split osteotomies are performed. This incision also
allows the maxillary tuberosity and pterygoid region to
be exposed.

CHAPTER 1

■ EXPOSURE OF THE ORBIT

Figure 1-14 I use a subciliary incision to gain access
to the floor of the orbit. I have not experienced complications with this approach. I begin the incision through
the skin and subcutaneous tissue and then angle it
downward through the orbicularis oculi muscle. On
the orbital rim, another incision is made through the
periosteum. This stair-step approach prevents subsequent scar contracture and other complications.
Figure 1-15 The stair-step incision is used to expose

the floor of the orbit. Extending the incision laterally
and medially allows exposure of the lateral and medial
walls of the orbit.
Figure 1-16 The orbital floor may also be exposed
through the transconjunctival approach. The incision
is made 4–6 mm below the lid margin and extends directly down to the orbital rim, where the periosteum is
transected, allowing access to the globe and bony orbit.

CHAPTER 1

■ INTRAORAL EXPOSURE OF THE
ASCENDING RAMUS
Figure 1-17 The ascending ramus is visualized and
palpated intraorally. An incision is made lateral to it in
the posterior buccal sulcus by using a Colorado needle
attached to an electrocautery unit. This incision is continued directly over the anterior border of the ascending ramus up to the level of the sigmoid notch.

Figure 1-18 By using a periosteal elevator, the ascending ramus and outer angle of the mandible are
completely exposed. This incision may be extended anteriorly when surgery on the mandible is planned.
Lighted retractors are used when performing surgical
procedures on the ascending ramus.

■ EXPOSURE OF THE CHIN
Figure 1-19 The incision for exposure of the chin is
carried through the labial mucosa and muscle to allow
for two-layer closure after surgery. The incision is carried to the bone, and the tissue is undermined subperiosteally. Special attention is paid to exposure of both
submental nerves. The tunneling technique prevents
avulsion of the submental nerve at the time of surgery.
Figure 1-20 The chin is exposed. The mental nerve is
protected by the retractor. The muscle remains at-

tached to the chin. By using a reciprocating saw, I perform the osteotomy for a sliding genioplasty. I avoid degloving the chin because it may result in redundancy or
lack of tone of the lower lip.
Figure 1-21 A cross-section view. After the chin is
slid forward, it is fixed with three wires at three separate locations. They are placed through the outer table
of the proximal segment to the inner table of the distal
segment. Advancement of the single-tiered sliding genioplasty is limited by the thickness of the bone.

■ FIXATION TECHNIQUES
LEFORT I ADVANCEMENT
Figure 1-22 After a Lefort I osteotomy, the maxillary
segments are immobilized with internal rigid fixation
using the Medicon CMS System. In this case, three bar
plates and a single L-shaped plate were used. Although
fixation does not completely eliminate relapse, it does
reduce the degree. Note the prefabricated acrylic splint,
which establishes occlusion in a predetermined position.

Figure 1-23 A close-up view of the placement of
plates and 5- to 7-mm length screws 1.2 mm in diameter. The bone must be drilled under a stream of saline
solution to avoid overheating the bone.

CHAPTER 1

LEFORT III ADVANCEMENT

Figure 1-28 The Lefort III is one of the most frequently used procedures in craniofacial surgery. It allows the orbits and midfacial skeleton to be mobilized
through either an extracranial approach or a combined
intracranial–extracranial approach. This procedure incorporates frontonasal disjunction allowing variations
for cutting the lateral orbit. The procedure requires interpositional bone grafting and rigid fixation. The bone
grafts, either autogenous or demineralized bone, may
be placed in the frontonasal area along the orbits and in
the zygomatic arches. I do not use bone grafts in the
pterygomaxillary area. Intermaxillary fixation, rigid
fixation, and bone grafts all complement one another in
stabilizing major midface advancements.
Figure 1-29 A titanium screw held by the center drive
is easy to use and provides good visibility for the surgeon.

Figure 1-30 Compression titanium plates are used for
rigid fixation of the fractured bone. The compression
plates are designed so that the screws, when tightened
at an angle, bring the bone fragments into close alignment. I routinely use noncompression titanium plates
in craniofacial fixation.
Figure 1-31 The screw inside the bone must be drawn
through the outer and inner cortical plates to achieve
rigid fixation.
Figure 1-32 Fixation of two bone segments is accomplished by using interosseous wiring. This method is

MEDIAL CANTHAL APPROACH
Figure 1-40 A skin incision is made to expose the medial canthal region. This technique was developed by
Fuente del Campo. A back cut below the lower lid allows
the straight line of the epicanthal fold to be broken up.
Figure 1-41 A transverse incision allows for the advancement of a small skin flap. The incision point is
determined by the location of the corner of the eye and
the optical level.

Figure 1-42 The incision is completed. The skin is
tightened when the epicanthal fold is pulled forward
with a finger.

opposite ligament with wire, as shown in the next series of drawings.

LATERAL CANTHOPEXY
One of the most frequently performed procedures
in craniofacial surgery is lateral canthopexy. The
lateral canthus must be adjusted 2–3 mm above the
level of the medial canthus.
Figure 1-48 The lateral canthus is normally attached
to the periosteum at the lateral tubercle of the orbit.
Frequently I use a permanent nylon suture to attach the
lateral canthus to the lateral orbital wall by drilling two
holes in the bone and tying the sutures over the bone.
Figure 1-49

The nylon suture is passed through the

lateral canthus. Two holes are drilled in the bone above
the frontal zygomatic suture.

Figure 1-50 To fix the lateral tendon in the appropriate position, the nylon sutures are passed through the
holes in the bone by using a wire loop.

Figure 1-51 The lateral tendon is tightly secured in
the new position by tying the suture over the lateral orbital rim. It is important to make sure the lateral canthus is at the same level as that on the opposite eye.

■ CARTILAGE AND BONE GRAFTS
Autogenous cartilage and bone remain an important source of grafting material in craniofacial and
cleft surgery. In my practice, I use auricular cartilage, septal cartilage and bone, rib cartilage and
bone, cancellous bone from the iliac crest, and

bone grafts from the cranium. A bone substitute
that I consider extremely valuable is demineralized perforated bone, produced by the Pacific
Coast Tissue Bank. This material is both osteoinductive and osteoconductive.

AURICULAR CONCHAL CARTILAGE
GRAFT
I use cartilage from the ear to augment the nose
when septal cartilage is not available. However,
auricular cartilage is not as good as septal cartilage
for nasal-tip support because it is curved and less
rigid. Auricular cartilage requires reinforcement
with a suture to create a columellar strut. Despite
this drawback, auricular cartilage is my second
choice for nasal-tip augmentation and even for
dorsal augmentation, when required.

Figure 1-66 Rib grafts are now rarely used in craniofacial surgery. I use a costochondral graft to create the

fifth or sixth intercostal areas. If necessary, a costochondral graft can be obtained.

CHAPTER 1

DEMINERALIZED BONE
Demineralized bone from the Pacific Coast Tissue
Bank is an alternative to cranial bone for grafting
material. Demineralized bone is osteoinductive
and easier to shape than cranial bone. Various
types of demineralized implants are available to
accommodate the various needs of reconstructive
surgery. The selection of the demineralized bone
implant depends on the size and location of the defect. I have used more than 1,400 demineralized
bone implants in the last 7 years and find them an
excellent bone substitute in craniofacial surgery.
Figure 1-85 When cranial bone is not available, I use
a cortical strip of demineralized perforated bone. The
perforations in the strip allow new bone ingrowth, and

the strips are pliable. I soak them in an antibiotic solution for 1 h before surgery.
Figure 1-86 Cortical perforated strips can be easily
shaped to follow the curve of the cranium.
Figure 1-87 The cortical strips are sutured in place
with resorbable sutures. New bone formation occurs
over a period of several months and is demonstrated by
the presence of osteocytes and osteoblasts. Note the zigzag skin incision in front of the demineralized bone
grafts. I avoid placing the demineralized bone grafts in
the line of the skin incision to avoid complications in
the healing of demineralized bone.

BASIC SURGICAL TECHNIQUES

Figure 1-87

CHAPTER 1

■ FIBRIN TISSUE ADHESIVES
Figure 1-94 I frequently use fibrin adhesives in craniofacial surgery. Two components are mixed to create
the fibrin tissue adhesive: (a) fibrinogen, which is obtained by spinning down a unit of blood from the patient and separating the fibrinogen from the plasma;
and (b) thrombin and CaCl2, which acts as a fibrinolysis inhibitor. I use the fibrin adhesive for sealing leaks
in the dura and mucosa, obliterating dead space,
achieving hemostasis, binding bone grafts, and as a pericranial graft adhesive.
Figure 1-95 Fibrin tissue adhesive is mixed with
bone dust or demineralized bone granules to form a
filler for burr holes or contour defects.

Figure 1-96 Bone paste is used for grafting in burr
holes or bone junctions.
Figure 1-97 A dural graft is sealed with a fibrin tissue
adhesive. It is placed along the suture line to prevent
cerebrospinal fluid leakage. See references on Fibrin
Tissue Adhesive for further information.
Figure 1-98 A burr hole in the cranium can be closed
with a titanium plate. I use the plate designed by Medicon CMS System. The titanium plate is fixed with small
0.9-mm diameter screws. The plate not only closes the
burr hole but aids in fixing the bony segment.

Figure 1-103 The concept of recurrent parabolic
shapes as a means for cutting, expanding, or shaping
bone was introduced by Hendle. This concept is based
on repeating ellipses, which allow expansion of the
bone as the curved cranial bone becomes flat. I use this
technique to change the shape of the bone in the cranial
vault and in other operations requiring a change in contour and shape.
Figure 1-104 Linear cuts are made in the bone to

shape.

of the bone allow a greater degree of expansion.
Figure 1-107 A similar technique of cuts as shown in
Figure 1-106 allows more expansion without the
drilled punctures.

CHAPTER 1

LAMELLAR-SPLIT OSTEOTOMY
I developed this technique to change the shape of
the outer table of a bone while keeping the inner
table in place, which serves as a reference point.
An interpositional bone graft can be placed between the tables. This technique is superior to the
onlay bone graft. I have not observed resorption of
the outer table. Furthermore, this technique can be
successfully used on the frontal bone, malar bone,
and in the orbital, maxillary, and mandibular regions. Depending on the location, it can be performed extracranially or intracranially.
Figure 1-114 Lamellar split of the malar bone is accomplished by using the coronal approach. The same
procedure can be performed by using an intraoral approach. The lamellar split is carried over to the level of

the inferior orbital nerve so that the entire zygomatic
arch can be changed in shape, projection, and width.
Figure 1-115 This view from above shows the proposed lamellar split (dotted line), which is carried to
the inferior orbital nerve.
Figure 1-116 The outer table of the malar bone is advanced after a lamellar-split osteotomy. An interpositional bone graft is inserted between the outer and inner tables to project the malar eminence.
Figure 1-117 The left side is corrected by using the
lamellar-split technique with the insertion of a bone
graft. On the right side, an interpositional bone graft is
ready for insertion.

SURGERY FOR
••••••••••••••••••••••

CRANIOSYNOSTOSIS
••••••••••••••••••••••

IN INFANTS

Kenneth E. Salyer
Daniel Marchac

SURGERY FOR CRANIOSYNOSTOSIS IN INFANTS

Craniofacial surgery has significantly improved
the treatment outcomes of craniosynostosis. New
techniques of remodeling and reshaping the cranial vault allow for rapid expansion and growth of
the brain. I prefer to operate on infants with craniosynostosis at the age of 6 months.
Craniofacial remodeling techniques were developed in the early 1970s. Marchac popularized the
floating-forehead procedure, whereas other surgeons used the tongue-in-groove method. These
methods offered a major advancement over strip
craniotomy or morselization. I developed the technique of total cranial-vault remodeling and have
found it to be the best approach for severe syndromal craniosynostosis and other extensive cranialvault deformities, such as severe plagiocephaly or
craniofacial scoliosis. My technique includes osteotomies in the anterior, middle, and posterior
cranial fossa, as well as in a portion or all of the
cranial vault.

65

A craniotomy is performed by a pediatric neurosurgeon to remove the deformed section of the cranium. Frequently a bifrontal craniotomy is performed, which may be expanded to remove the
entire cranial vault and allow osteotomies to be
performed in the cranial base. The skeletal segments are reshaped and replaced into new positions by using interosseous wire fixation, resorbable sutures, or miniplates.
Simple craniosynostosis resulting in plagiocephaly, brachycephaly, or trigonocephaly and
other related deformities can be successfully
treated with frontocranial remodeling. Lambdoid
synostosis, which rarely requires surgical correction, may be treated with occipital bandeau remodeling. I treat syndromal synostosis in complex
deformities such as Crouzon’s, Apert’s, Saethre–Chotzen, Pfeiffer, and Kleeblatschädel syndromes
with total cranial remodeling.

66

■ PLAGIOCEPHALY
Plagiocephaly is a descriptive term derived from
the Greek plagio, meaning oblique or slanted, and
cephalo, meaning head. Synostotic plagiocephaly
results from premature closure of a unilateral
coronal suture and may also involve a unilateral
lambdoidal suture. In rare cases, the premature
closure involves only an isolated lambdoidal suture.
Simple plagiocephaly can be corrected by using
either a unilateral or bilateral procedure. I prefer to
use a bilateral procedure even in minor deformities because better symmetry can be obtained. I
modified the unilateral procedure, originally described by Hoffman, in the following ways.
Figure 2-15 The unilateral frontal craniotomy, as described by Hoffman. It is designed at the level of the
coronal suture and extends into the midline. Forehead

and bandeau advancement correct the asymmetry.
Three burr holes are generally used for the craniotomy.
The hemibandeau is also reshaped and advanced forward.

Figure 2-16 Advancement of the bony segment leaves
a defect along the coronal suture. In infants, this defect
fills with new bone.

Figure 2-17 A view from above of the deformity and
design of the operation.

Figure 2-18 Viewed from above, the forehead and
hemibandeau have been advanced by using a tonguein-groove technique. The bony defect is evident along
the coronal suture.

CHAPTER 2

■ BRACHYCEPHALY
BILATERAL CORONAL SYNOSTOSIS
Brachycephaly is frequent in syndromal cases in
association with other suture involvement and is
seldom encountered as simple coronal synostosis.
Closure of the coronal sutures results in an abnormal vertical height of the forehead with supraorbital retrusion. Frequently more than just the front
of the head is affected, in which case, total cranialvault remodeling may be indicated. In minor deformities, remodeling with a bandeau and frontoorbital remodeling may suffice.
Figure 2-32 Design of total cranial-vault remodeling
in a case of brachycephaly. A bifrontal craniotomy is
performed by removing the frontal bone in one piece
and removing the parietal occipital bones in halves.
The bandeau is removed just above the frontonasal
junction and above the orbits; the orbital roof is cut
from the inside out, protecting the frontal lobes. The
proposed orbital osteotomies may extend into the inferior orbital fissure and around the front of the orbit, to
allow for greenstick advancement.
Figure 2-33 Total cranial-vault remodeling: All segments have been removed, cut, and reshaped to allow
for rotation and optimal recontouring. The supraorbital
bandeau has been cut and advanced. The osteotomies

in the orbits, extending intracranially to the inferior orbital fissure, are completed. The osteotomy extends
through the malar bone, where the lateral inferior orbit
can be greensticked anteriorly. When performed bilaterally, the bandeau and orbits can be advanced.
Figure 2-34 The entire cranial vault is reshaped and
remodeled. The orbits are advanced and extended laterally. Two occipitoparietal segments are reshaped and
changed in position. Interosseous wiring is used for fixation.
Figure 2-35 Preoperative 3-D computed tomography
(CT) reconstruction in a case of plagiocephaly.
Figure 2-36 Postoperative 3-D CT 2 years after the
surgical procedure, showing total cranial-vault remodeling.
Figure 2-37 Preoperative frontal view of a 6-monthold patient with brachycephaly with bicoronal synostosis.
Figure 2-38 Postoperative frontal view of the same
patient 1 year after total cranial-vault remodeling.

■ SCAPHOCEPHALY
Scaphocephaly is premature closure of the sagittal
sutures, which may or may not include the
metopic suture. This condition results in a long,
narrow skull. The surgical correction of scaphocephaly may be performed at any age, but it is easier when performed at 6 weeks of age. When performed at an older age, the skull is thicker, and a
more complex procedure is required.

anteriorly to shorten the anteroposterior dimension of
the skull.

Figure 2-52 A segment of bone is removed transversely from the front and back of the head to shorten
the length, whereas the two temporoparietal bones are
widened. This technique allows the bitemporal width
to be expanded. Alternatively, the bone marked for removal can be used in the midline to increase the width
of the skull.

■ LAMBDOID SYNOSTOSIS
True isolated unilateral lambdoid synostosis is
rare and must be differentiated from positional
plagiocephaly, which does not require surgical intervention. The surgical treatment of this condition is similar to that used in plagiocephaly or
brachycephaly, but the bandeau is used in the posterior occipital region. The parietal bones are reshaped and replaced. In lambdoid synostosis, the
bandeau and occipital bone are removed from the
occipital region and reshaped to create a normallooking skull. If the forehead is involved, it is corrected in a second procedure.

■ KLEEBLATSCHÄDEL DEFORMITY
This deformity results from premature synostosis
of all the cranial sutures in utero. Although rare,
Kleeblatschädel deformity is an acute condition
requiring immediate surgical intervention to release the compressed brain and allow expansion.
The following case demonstrates this deformity in
a 7-day-old infant.
Figure 2-87 Kleeblatschädel synostosis with closure
of the sutures and compression of the brain, which is
eroding the skull. Segments of removed bone reveal
holes caused by the compressed and eroding brain.

DEFORMITIES

PRIMARY AND SECONDARY SURGERY FOR CRANIAL AND FOREHEAD DEFORMITIES

111

■ CRANIAL-VAULT REMODELING
Cranial-vault remodeling provides expansion or reduction of the cranial volume. Cranial-vault expansion is indicated when increased intracranial pressure produces clinical symptoms. Craniosynostosis
and hydrocephalus are the most frequent causes of
increased intracranial pressure. Various tumors as
well as fibrous dysplasia may have the same effect.
Cranial-vault reconstruction involves reshaping
the proportions of the deformed cranium to achieve
normal shape and a better balance between the cranium and face. The shape and size of the anterior,
middle, and posterior cranial fossa also can be
changed, improving the shape and balance of the
head and face.
The key to total cranial-vault reconstruction is
the use of the forehead bandeau, a concept that I
have applied to other areas of the cranial vault.
The bandeau allows the angle of the forehead, or-

bital, and frontonasal angle disproportions to be
improved, and anterior and middle cranial-base
abnormalities to be expanded or changed. The
midcranial-vault bandeau is helpful in adjusting
the vertical height of the cranium. The occipital
bandeau changes the height and posterior projection in the occipital region and corrects deformities of the posterior cranial vault and fossa. Applying these techniques for total cranial-vault
expansion in syndromal craniosynostosis improves results in the most difficult cases. The application of the same techniques to cranial-vault
reduction has opened new possibilities for treating
children with hydrocephalus. Head reductions
have allowed these patients to hold up their heads
and walk. In this chapter, I present techniques I
have developed and used for cranial-vault reduction and expansion.

112

CHAPTER 3

Figure 3-1 The bandeaus are designed and cut while
the intracranial and orbital structures are protected.
They are then removed, remodeled, and replaced, providing structural stability and symmetry and guiding
the changes in cranial-vault proportions and volume.
The adjacent bones below and above the bandeau are
cut and expanded to meet the new shape and position
of the three bandeaus. The fronto-orbital osteotomy below the bandeau allows bitemporal expansion. Posteriorly, a barrel-stave technique is used to expand or reduce the posterior cranial fossa and head.
Figure 3-2 View from above. Cranial-vault expansion
demonstrates how the use of the bandeau allows
changes in the shape and volume of the cranial vault.
Figure 3-3 A frontonasal angle of 90⬚ is the lower
limit of what I consider aesthetically pleasing.

Figure 3-4 The upper limit is ⬃120⬚. The goal in each
case is to achieve a frontonasal angle between 90 and
120⬚, depending on the face, the deformity, anticipated
growth, and age of the patient.

Figure 3-5 The bandeau is turned over, which allows
the frontonasal angle to be adjusted. The supraorbital
rim should be 8–10 mm in front of the cornea of the eye
in the adult face.

Figure 3-6 The bandeau, which has been turned
⬎180⬚, is advanced and repositioned. This method is
one of the many that I use for changing the frontonasal
angle. Other designs are presented in this and other
chapters.

CRANIAL-VAULT EXPANSION FOR
PATIENTS WITH HYDROCEPHALUS
WITH FUNCTIONING SHUNTS WITH
INTRACRANIAL HYPERTENSION
AND SLIT-VENTRICLE SYNDROME
The following two cases demonstrate cranial-vault
expansion techniques.
Figure 3-7 Preoperative frontal view of a 6-year-old
patient who has a shunt-dependent hydrocephalus.
She developed slit-ventricle syndrome because of increased intracranial pressure and had severe
headaches, which were refractory to conventional
treatment. To alleviate the symptoms, the pediatric
neurosurgeon and I planned to expand the size of her
skull. Previously she had undergone bitemporal decompression craniectomies by a neurosurgeon, without
success.
Figure 3-8 Preoperative submental vertex view
shows unsuccessful bitemporal decompression, resulting in bitemporal bulging.
Figure 3-9 Lateral computed axial tomography (CT)
scan demonstrates previous unsuccessful temporal decompression with evidence of the ventriculoperitoneal
shunt located posteriorly on the right.
Figure 3-10 The cranial vault is expanded by forming
two anterior segments and two posterior segments,
which are removed parasagittally.

Figure 3-11 The design shown from above. Two burr
holes are drilled to remove four segments of cranial
vault. Once the dura is freed, a craniotome is used to
cut the segments.
Figure 3-12 Lateral view of the cranial-vault expansion
with fixation of the bony segments. Bone grafts fill the
spaces, including the site of previous decompression.
Figure 3-13 The operation has been completed, as
seen from above. The patient had a shunt in place,
which put her at risk for collapse postoperatively if
pressure variations occurred because of the shunt. The
newly expanded shape of the skull was maintained
with internal rigid fixation to improve the stability of
the reconstructed cranial vault while healing.
Figure 3-14 The removed segments of bone were split
and used to bone-graft the spaces.
Figure 3-15 Intraoperative view demonstrates how,
before final bone grafting, titanium plates are used for
fixation in the expansion of the cranial vault. This procedure was completely successful in eliminating the
patient’s headaches; furthermore, it left no external evidence of surgical intervention.

CHAPTER 3

VARIATION ON CRANIAL-VAULT
EXPANSION
The following variation of the previous technique
was used for the correction of hydrocephalus with
functioning shunt with increased intracranial
pressure, which produced slit-ventricle syndrome.
The posterior three-fourths of the cranium was expanded, correcting this functional deformity.
Figure 3-16 Frontal view of a 9-year-old patient with
symptoms of intracranial hypertension in the presence
of a functional shunt. Monitoring of intracranial pressure revealed that it was increased, causing headaches.
Computerized tomography scans demonstrated small
ventricles. The following figures show the design used
to expand his calvarium.
Figure 3-17 Lateral view of the design indicates an
extension of bone around the shunt where it enters the
dura. The pericranium is sutured around the tubing of
the shunt to secure it in place. Bilateral temporoparietooccipital flaps are designed parasagittally.

Figure 3-18 Three bony segments of the skull are removed, including the strip over the sagittal sinus. The
two larger segments of calvarial bone are split and used
for expansion grafting.

Figure 3-21. All segments of bone have been replaced, expanding the cranial vault laterally and posteriorly. Multiplate internal rigid fixation prevents potential collapse if pressure variation occurs during the
early postoperative period.

CHAPTER 3

■ ARNOLD–CHIARI MALFORMATION
SYNDROME ASSOCIATED WITH
HYDROCEPHALUS
The concept of an acquired Chiari I malformation
has been reported in the literature (Venes et al.). At
our Center, we have seen this condition in association with Crouzon’s syndrome and Apert’s syndrome, in addition to hydrocephalus. These patients are at risk for cerebellar tonsillar herniation
(Chiari I malformation), which may account for
motor deficits and developmental delays seen in
this group of patients. Magnetic resonance imaging
(MRI) should be performed preoperatively to determine the presence of hydrocephalus and possibly of Chiari I malformation.
The craniofacial surgical techniques I use in the
correction of this deformity are illustrated in the
following case of a 13-year-old patient with hydrocephalus and shunt-induced cephalocranial disproportion with Chiari I malformation.
Figure 3-22 Two parasagittal bone segments are designed for removal, whereas two temporoparietal flaps
of bone are designed for cranial-vault expansion laterally. The abnormal posterior occipital cap of bone is removed to the level of the foramen magnum. Cervical
decompression was performed by the pediatric neurosurgeon at the time of this procedure.
Figure 3-23 One of the two paramedian blocks of
bone is removed, split, and used for cranial-vault expansion. It shows marked variation in thickness.
Figure 3-24 Frontal view demonstrates bitemporoparietal cranial-vault expansion. After a greenstick-fracture osteotomy, fixation is achieved with split
cranial bone segments.

Figure 3-25 The temporoparietal bone segments are
expanded laterally to increase the volume of the head.
An osteotomy is performed by using a greenstick-fracture technique. The occipital cap is removed, expanded, flattened with cuts, and inserted to shorten the
anteroposterior length of the skull after cervical decompression was performed.

■ CRANIAL-VAULT REDUCTION FOR

Children with hydrocephalus exhibit developmental delays related to an inability to lift their
heads and walk. I have developed cranial-vault reduction techniques that have proven to be beneficial for these children. The following two cases
demonstrate these techniques.

Figure 3-45 Anteroposterior expansion and narrowing of the cranium are shown from above. By using various reshaped bony segments, a more normal-shaped
skull is created. Internal rigid fixation is used; a peninsula of bone attachment around the shunt is maintained with pericranial suturing around the tubing to
secure the tube when the cranial vault is rebuilt.

Figure 3-42 The shape of the head of a patient with
hydrocephalus. Marked widening with severe brachycephaly is noted; the head circumference is 61.9 cm. As
shown in this frontal view, the proposed design of the
cuts will allow bitemporal narrowing of the cranium
with total reduction of the cranial vault and improvement in the anterior projection in the frontoorbital region.

Figure 3-46 Preoperative frontal view of a 20-monthold patient with severe hydrocephalus. Because of her
head’s massive size, the patient was unable to lift it off
the pillow. The surgical indication for cranial-vault reduction involved increasing the patient’s mobility and
improving aesthetics.

Figure 3-43 Lateral view of the design of the procedure to reduce and remodel the cranial vault. The reduction will occur bitemporally, and there will be a
marked narrowing of the brachycephalic orientation
with an increase in the anteroposterior direction.
Figure 3-44 The segments have been removed, cut,
reshaped, and replaced, providing anteroposterior
lengthening, narrowing of the cranium, and height reduction. The presence of the shunt creates a postoperative risk of collapse if marked pressure changes occur
before healing is complete; thus internal rigid fixation
is used.

previously described. The patient’s aesthetics and ability to lift her head improved.

I use in patients who have had previous inadequate
surgery or who have never had surgical correction.

Figure 3-71 The abnormally shaped bandeau is removed and reshaped to be inserted in the occipital region.
Figure 3-72 After the occipital cap is removed, it is

can be advanced anteriorly to shorten the anteroposterior length of the skull.

Figure 3-69 The design for surgery of this scaphocephalic skull includes the use of barrel-stave expansion at the base of the skull in the temporoparietal region. A craniotomy allows the removal of bones,
including a bandeau from the biparietal region, which

shortening in the anteroposterior dimension and

removed, flattened, and broadened to allow reshaping
of the posterior skull to create two parietal eminences.

and the two interlocking parietotemporal segments are
inserted in an expanded position.

segments are removed, reshaped, and replaced, creating normal cranial-vault symmetry.

RECONSTRUCTION

Kenneth E. Salyer

ORBITAL SURGERY, HYPERTELORISM, AND NASAL RECONSTRUCTION

Tessier was the first to demonstrate that correction
of hypertelorism and translocation of the globe
could be successfully performed with orbital osteotomies through the intracranial approach. The
development and refinement of orbital surgery established craniofacial surgery as a new subspecialty. The highly complex orbital region consists
of the bony orbit, the globe, the eyelids, and softtissue adnexal structures. Therefore, the surgical
procedures aimed at transposition of the entire orbit require precise planning, excellent knowledge
of anatomic structures, and highly developed surgical skills. The frequent use of the intracranial approach requires close cooperation with an experienced neurosurgeon.
The most intricate procedure performed in the
orbital region is correction of hypertelorism. In the
normal adult, the interorbital distance measures
⬃25 mm. Any measurement ⬎35 mm is considered abnormal. However, in my practice, I have declined to operate on many patients with wider interorbital distances because the overall facial
appearance was appealing. The interorbital distance in children must be evaluated on a case-bycase basis.
When correcting hypertelorism, I strongly suggest overcorrecting the interorbital bony distance
to 18–20 mm because the soft tissue, once re-

137

draped and healed, appears wider than it does on
the operating table.
I frequently use orbital osteotomies to solve major problems in the orbital region, especially in patients with congenital craniofacial deformities. Reducing the interorbital distance for correction of
hypertelorism involves translocating the entire
bony orbit and its contents. The complications of
this procedure may be severe, resulting in exophthalmos or enophthalmos, but can be prevented by
following the details of the surgical procedures
outlined in this chapter. Correction of these complications also is described and illustrated. Another complication of this surgery is damage to the
nasolacrimal system. In my experience, this complication can be prevented by unroofing the
lacrimal sac and duct during surgery.
Because hypertelorism is almost always associated with nasal deformity, the correction of this
deformity is an integral part of my approach. It involves reconstruction of all nasal structures, bone,
cartilage, and soft tissue, with the aim of achieving
an aesthetically appealing nose.
This chapter also includes a description of the
surgical techniques for correction of frontonasal
encephalocele, which is associated with a long
nose.

■ CORRECTION OF

Figure 4-1 The globe and orbit may be translocated in
any direction. The appropriate orientation of the orbit—and especially the globe—must be maintained to
avoid postoperative complications. The bandeau provides the necessary orientation for the anteroposterior
(AP) positioning of the translocated orbit.
Figure 4-2 The ideal relation of the bony orbit and the
globe is achieved when the anterior part of the cornea
in the adult is 8–10 mm behind the supraorbital bony
rim. This condition can be achieved with surgical cor-

rection by changing the position of the supraorbital
bandeau or the position of the globe or both. The globe
may be translocated after moving one, two, three, or all
four walls of the orbit.
Figure 4-3 Note the position of the globe in reference to
the lateral and medial orbital walls. In translocation of the
bony orbit, these relations must be kept in mind. In correction of hypertelorism, the osteotomy must be placed
far enough in the orbit to allow optimal translocation of
the globe. This distance is, in my experience, ⬃2 cm.

CHAPTER 4

■ ORBITAL AND NASOORBITAL
SEGMENTAL OSTEOTOMIES
This series of drawings demonstrates some of the
segmental and nasoorbital osteotomies used in reconstructive procedures within and around the
bony orbit. The goal of the procedure is to change
the orbital contour and size. The various techniques used to achieve this goal are presented as
separate procedures or are incorporated into other
surgical techniques.
Figure 4-11 The upper half of the orbit has been cut
down to the inferior orbital fissure, including the orbital roof and upper medial orbital quadrant. Translocation of this segment can be accomplished by removing bone in the interorbital area. This segment can also
be rotated to change the shape and form of the orbit.
Figure 4-12 The malar orbital rim is cut up to the
midportion of the lateral orbital wall, including the nasolacrimal fossa and the nasal bone. This procedure or
a variation of it allows the lateral orbital wall to be
moved laterally or inferiorly. This procedure is frequently used in secondary cases of trauma when the
malar bone has been fractured or when there is a tripod
or other orbital fracture inadequately reduced. The segment can be moved up, down, or laterally.

Figure 4-13 The frontal nasal bone is moved, which
can affect the shape and position of the orbit, globe, and
nose. This procedure is frequently performed in combination with a Lefort III osteotomy, in which there is
not only frontonasal disjunction but also inclusion of
the maxilla and malar bones. This technique or a modification of it can be used to lengthen the nose and is
discussed later in the chapter.
Figure 4-14 Segmental osteotomy of the upper inner
quadrant of the orbit is frequently performed in the correction of hypertelorism. Frequently this region is inadequately corrected in hypertelorism because not
enough bone is remodeled in the interorbital area.
Figure 4-15 Classic medial translocation of the entire
bony orbit is illustrated. The cuts in the temporal fossa
allow the entire orbit to be moved medially after removal of the medial orbital segment. This procedure
should be performed only when the surgeon determines that the shape and size of the orbit is satisfactory.
Figure 4-16 In microphthalmos, the orbit can be expanded by using segmental osteotomies of all four
walls.

CHAPTER 4

■ MEDIAL CANTHAL LIGAMENT
IDENTIFICATION
Correction of hypertelorism, as well as many other
orbital procedures, begins with identification of
the medial canthal tendon.
Figure 4-17 The incision extends along the epicanthal fold to its inferior point; from there, a small back
cut is made toward the lower lid. The two points indicated on the drawing will be brought together at the
time of skin closure.

Figure 4-18 Precise dissection of the soft tissue allows the medial canthal tendon to be identified. In correction of hypertelorism, the tendon is tagged with a
wire suture for transnasal medial canthopexy, which is
performed at the end of the hypertelorism procedure.
Figure 4-19 When the medial canthopexy is completed, the skin is closed, and the two points, indicated
in the drawings, are brought together to prevent the formation of an epicanthal fold.

■ INTRACRANIAL CORRECTION OF
HYPERTELORISM
For correction of hypertelorism, I use a classic intracranial four-wall osteotomy. To correct minor
degrees of this deformity, I use variations of the
procedure with an extracranial approach.
Figure 4-20 Bilateral complete ethmoidectomy allows
medial four-wall translocation of both orbits. I have not
observed relapse in patients with this technique.
Figure 4-21 After the neurosurgeon performs a
bifrontal craniotomy, I use a reciprocating saw to

perform the osteotomy through an intracranial approach. The extent of intracranial exposure depends
on the degree of hypertelorism. The anterior cribriform plate is dissected free, medially. If this area is
severely deformed or abnormally wide, it must be resected to allow medial translocation of the orbits.
Frequently the cribriform plate can be left, preserving the olfactory function. Intracranial osteotomies
require more precision than those performed through
the orbital approach. The orbital approach allows the
surgeon to better visualize the relation of the cuts to
the orbit.

along the front of the maxilla. The soft tissue and infraorbital nerve must be protected. Lighted retractors
facilitate both exposure and the osteotomy in the orbital and nasal regions.

Figure 4-28 I use the technique demonstrated in this

attention to protecting the orbital contents and identifying the inferior orbital fissure.

CHAPTER 4

■ FACIAL BIPARTITION PROCEDURE
This procedure was developed by Van der Muelen
and Tessier. Ortiz-Monasterio contributed the details of the planning of the operation. The procedure is indicated under specific conditions of hypertelorism with true lateral rotation of the orbits
and a V-shaped occlusal plane. Correction of this
deformity requires bilateral orbital maxillary osteotomies with a facial bipartition and subsequent
rotation of the segments medially and downward.

The initial incision is made at the midline with a reciprocating saw. The surgeon then places a finger on
the palate and, with a thin, straight osteotome, splits
the bony palate. The remaining osteotomies are performed as described earlier in the chapter. Mobilization
of the pterygomaxillary area is described in chapter 5.
The configuration of the bandeau is changed to accommodate changes in the frontonasal contour after moving the two halves of the face into their new positions.
Bone grafts fill the created defects.

Figure 4-69 Hypertelorism with a V-shaped occlusal
plane and maxillary deformity. The darkly shaded area
indicates the amount of bone to be removed to bring the
orbital maxillary segments medially and downward.
According to Ortiz-Monasterio, the markings indicate
the proposed angle of rotation of the orbit and maxilla.

Figure 4-72 Preoperative frontal view of a 15-year-old
patient with a midfacial cleft and hypertelorism, a bifid
nose, and a deformed maxilla.

Figure 4-70 The design of cuts for a bifrontal craniotomy and forehead bandeau allows an intracranial
approach to reposition the orbits and maxillary segments. The shaded area in the previous drawing shows
a completed V-shaped resection at the midline.
Figure 4-71 After the bipartition procedure, the bony
segments, including the orbital maxillary segments, are
newly positioned and fixed with wires. The osteotomy
at the midline of the palate requires special attention.

Figure 4-73 Postoperative frontal view of the same
patient 3 years after bipartition correction of the face
and hypertelorism.

Figure 4-74 Preoperative submental vertex view
showing a lack of nasal projection of the bifid nose.

Figure 4-75 Postoperative submental vertex view of
the same patient after reconstruction of the nose and
lip. Note the projection of the nasal tip.

■ FACIAL BIPARTITION WITH
ADVANCEMENT
The facial bipartition procedure may be combined
with the monoblock intracranial advancement.
The procedure poses a greater risk of infection due
to the advancement of both the frontal orbital region and the maxilla. It is rarely indicated.

correct the rotational discrepancy in leveling the occlusal plane. These maneuvers allow medial and
downward rotation of the orbital maxillary segments
and adjustment of the occlusion. An additional bone
graft is placed in the frontal orbital region, where rotation of the orbits leaves a defect.

■ LAMELLAR-SPLIT CORRECTION OF
FRONTONASAL DYSPLASIA WITH
HYPERTELORISM
In 1987 I developed the lamellar-split osteotomy to
correct frontonasal dysplasia with hypertelorism.
The essence of the procedure lies in the ability to
change the shape and form of the outer table while
keeping the inner table in a fixed position. Initially
I used this technique to change the shape and form
in the frontal orbital region, but over time I began
to use it in other regions of the facial skeleton as
well. The procedure can be used only in patients
older than 3 years and in whom the bone is ⱖ4–5
mm thick.
Figure 4-78 Lamellar-split osteotomy is performed
for correction of frontonasal dysplasia causing hypertelorism. Note that the outer and inner tables of the
frontoorbit–zygomatic–maxillary region are split in
situ by using the intracranial approach. In this way the
shape of the entire area can be changed.
Figure 4-79 Preoperative view from above at the level
of the forehead bandeau. The frontal lobes are protected as I use the reciprocating saw to split the outer
table from the inner table.
Figure 4-80 The outer table is split, reshaped, and advanced, whereas the inner table remains in place as a
reference point. Bone grafts placed between the inner
and outer tables change the shape and contour of the
outer table.

Figure 4-81 The operation is completed. The forehead has been advanced and reconstructed; the segments have been reshaped. The segments and the interpositional bone grafts are fixed with 0.9-mm
titanium screws from the CMS system. By using the
lamellar-split osteotomy technique, the facial skeleton
can be subtly reshaped and the contour of the soft tissue can be changed to enhance overall facial appearance.
Figure 4-82 Preoperative right oblique view of a 5year-old patient with frontonasal dysplasia hypertelorism with a retruded short nose, forehead frontal
bossing, and lack of supraorbital rim on the right side.
Figure 4-83 Postoperative right oblique view of the
same patient after correction of hypertelorism and frontonasal dysplasia by using the lamellar-split technique.
Figure 4-84 Preoperative frontal view of the same patient.
Figure 4-85 Postoperative frontal view of the same
patient 7 years after correction of hypertelorism and
frontonasal dysplasia. The lamellar-split technique
and V-Y advancement and cranial bone grafting for
nasal reconstruction were performed. Note the improvement in the malar regions due to the split lamellar technique.

■ CORRECTION OF
HYPERTELORISM BY USING
THE “INVERTED-U” TECHNIQUE
AND THE LAMELLAR SPLIT
I developed this procedure to preserve the toothbaring areas in young children undergoing osteotomies for correction of hypertelorism. The
lamellar-split technique in the correction of hypertelorism allows the shape of the orbit to be
changed. The use of this technique depends on the
severity of hypertelorism and the age of the patient.
Figure 4-86 The inverted-U osteotomy is performed
through the intracranial approach. This procedure can
be performed with or without a forehead bandeau. The
osteotomies involve the medial orbital wall. The nasolacrimal duct and sac are exposed and must be protected during the osteotomy. The entire supraorbital
rim and roof of the orbit are included with a temporal
extension as a small bandeau to stabilize the orbits in a
new position. The osteotomy of the lateral orbital rim
may be extended as far as the floor of the orbit and may
vary, depending on the existing deformity and shape of
the lateral orbital wall.
Figure 4-87 The orbits are cut and transposed medially into their new positions. The irregular edges of the
bone are smoothed with a burr before fixation. A bilateral medial canthopexy is performed, as described earlier in the chapter. The defect in the left temporal area
is filled with bone grafts.
Figure 4-88 An interlocking bone technique of the
lateral orbit allows the upper segment of the orbit to be
fixed with interosseous wiring inside the inferior segment, assuring medial positioning of the orbit. This
close-up view demonstrates the adjustment and wiring
of the bone grafts to fit the defect.
Figure 4-89 The orbit may be removed if the osteotomy stops medial to the lacrimal fossa, leaving the

lacrimal duct intact. The reciprocating saw is placed
between the inner and outer tables, splitting the outer
table to change the shape of the orbit.

■ INTRACRANIAL MEDIAL-ORBITAL
WALL CORRECTION OF
HYPERTELORISM
This technique is primarily used for correction of
first-degree hypertelorism in which the lateral orbital wall and rim do not need to be moved to
achieve good results.
Figure 4-97 Design of a bifrontal craniotomy for an
intracranial approach to orbital cuts. The orbital cuts
incorporate a sliding supraorbital extension, which allows the glabellar region to be narrowed. In this technique, the central block of bone is removed and the
bony segment—including the supraorbital rim and medial orbital wall—is transposed medially. In my experience, the narrowing of the intraorbital distance
should be overcorrected, especially in adults. I recommend doing so especially in this procedure because
only part of the orbit is translocated.
Figure 4-98 The bony segments are translocated medially and fixed with interosseous wiring. The medial
and lateral canthi are repositioned. I use this technique
without bone grafting, although on occasion I add a cranial bone graft in the lateral orbit to move and maintain
the globe in a more medial position.

■ SUBCRANIAL MEDIAL-ORBITAL
WALL CORRECTION OF
HYPERTELORISM
This procedure is performed in lesser degrees of
hypertelorism in which the lateral orbital wall and
rim are not displaced.

Figure 4-110 Preoperative frontal view of a 14month-old patient with hypertelorism secondary to a
medial facial cleft.

Figure 4-108 The surgical design of the extracranial
procedure is illustrated. The medial canthal tendons
are isolated as described earlier in this chapter. The orbits are exposed through a bicoronal scalp incision.
The medial bone cuts are made after the medial orbital
structures are completely mobilized. The medial block
of bone must be cut below the level of the cribriform
plate to avoid injuring the dura. A CT scan with frontoorbital reformatting, as well as an AP roentgenogram,
is helpful for precise planning of the procedure. The
cut in the medial orbital wall is performed behind the
lacrimal fossa to preserve the lacrimal duct and sac. Inferiorly, the osteotomy reaches the pyriform aperture.
A partial submucous resection of the cartilaginous septum is usually performed to allow adequate nasal passage for breathing after removing the orbital wall medially. An extensive bilateral subcranial ethmoidectomy
is performed after mobilization of the medial-orbital
wall osteotomy.

Figure 4-111 Postoperative frontal view 6 years after
correction of hypertelorism by using the extracranial
approach described.

Figure 4-109 After I complete the osteotomies and
transpose the nasal bones medially, I secure the nasal
bones with internal rigid fixation. If necessary, bone
grafts may be used to fill in gaps.

Figure 4-112 Postoperative 3-D CT of a 12-year-old
patient 10 years after correction of hypertelorism with
a recurrence due to pneumatization of the ethmoid sinuses. This complication can be prevented if the ethmoid sinuses are removed, as demonstrated earlier in
the chapter, instead of infracturing the medial-orbital
walls.
Figure 4-113 Postoperative frontal view of a 10-yearold patient with pneumatization of the ethmoid sinuses and exorbitism, which is more severe on the
right side.
Figure 4-114 Postoperative frontal view of the same
patient after correction of hypertelorism, a lateral
lower osteotomy, bilateral medial and lateral canthopexy, and correction of exorbitism on the right
side.

■ INTRACRANIAL CORRECTION OF
FRONTONASAL ENCEPHALOCELE
The nasal deformity in frontonasal encephalocele
produces a long nose. Surgically correcting this
deformity primarily depends on achieving a good
reconstruction of the nose. The increase in interorbital bony distance is without true lateralization of
the orbits, which occurs in true hypertelorism.
That means the lateral orbital walls and rims are of
a normal distance apart without displacement.
Surgical correction can be achieved with medial
orbital surgery, which may include medial-wall
osteotomy and reshaping with bone grafts, nasal
cartilage, and soft-tissue reconstruction.

neurosurgeon excises the excess neural or dural sac extrusion and closes the dura at the base of the nose.

Figure 4-115 A bifrontal craniotomy is the surgical
approach to the frontonasal encephalocele. The
bifrontal craniotomy bone segment can be split and
used as a bone source for reconstruction of the orbit and
nose. The intracranial approach affords protection of
the brain as the encephalocele sac is separated from the
nasal soft tissues and adjacent structures, which facilitates isolation of the neck of the encephalocele from the
dura. Frequently excess nonfunctional neural tissue
may be in the sac; this possibility can be determined frequently by the preoperative CT and MRI. The pediatric

Figure 4-118 Intraoperative view after surgical closure of the dural defect and reconstruction of the medial
orbital wall and nasal dorsum with split cranial bone.

Figure 4-116 Reconstruction of the bony orbital and
nasal defect is performed by using split cranial bone
taken from the frontal craniotomy bone segment. Details of the nasal reconstruction are described later in
this chapter.

Figure 4-119 Postoperative frontal view 1 year after
surgical correction.
Figure 4-120 Long-term follow-up at age 12 years after single procedure for correction of encephalocele.
Patient required no further surgery.

■ INTRACRANIAL CORRECTION OF
HYPERTELORISM WITH
ENCEPHALOCELE BY USING
FOUR-WALL OSTEOTOMY
The following procedure involves an orbital osteotomy of four walls plus encephalocele correction. Such a procedure may be necessary when
true lateralization of the orbits exist with an encephalocele. This is an unusual deformity.
Figure 4-127 Design of the proposed osteotomy sites
with a bifrontal craniotomy, frontal bandeau, and proposed resection of the increased interorbital distance.

■ NASAL RECONSTRUCTION IN
HYPERTELORISM
Hypertelorism is most frequently associated with a
short-nose deformity. Correction of this deformity
may be the most important and difficult part in the
correction of hypertelorism. It may involve the reconstruction of all the nasal structures, including
bone, cartilage, and soft tissue. I frequently use the
following procedures in the order of preference:
local tissue, V-Y advancement, or forehead flap.
The following series is an example of hypertelorism correction by using local tissue for reconstruction of the nasal deformity.
Figure 4-133 Hypertelorism associated with a midline cleft of the lip, macrostomia, lateral clefting of the

the left ascending ramus and condyle.

an alar pedicle advancement for correction of the nasal
clefting.
Figure 4-135 A hook is placed on the nasal skin,
which has been totally undermined. The displaced alar
cartilage is dissected free from the overlying skin and is
mobilized on the lining and moved inferiorly to correct
the nasal-lining defect.

rotation advancement closure of the cleft in the skin.

CHAPTER 4

■ V-TO-INVERTED-Y LENGTHENING
OF THE CONGENITALLY
SHORT NOSE
I have used V-to-inverted-Y lengthening on the
congenitally short nose with frequent success. In
contrast to many surgeons who have not found this
procedure to be effective, I have had good results
when it is performed in the following manner.
The incision is designed so that forehead or
glabellar skin is used as the V to Y advancement,
which allows lengthening of the nasal skin. The incision extends paranasally to the alar base. The
skin over the entire nose is undermined, which allows the insertion of bone or cartilage implants (or
both) to lengthen the nose and improve its shape.
Figure 4-170 Preoperative frontal view of a patient
with hypertelorism and right microophthalmos with
increased interorbital distance and nasal deformity due
to frontonasal dysplasia.
Figure 4-171 Postoperative frontal view at age 21
years, 13 years after surgery by using V-Y nasal advancement with bone and cartilage grafting. A Mustardé lower eyelid to the upper eyelid switch was performed as well as reconstruction of the lower eyelid
with advancement of the cheek flap. A prosthetic eye is
present on the right.

CHAPTER 4

■ FRONTONASAL OSTEOTOMY FOR
LENGTHENING THE NOSE
This procedure, reported recently in the literature
by Wolfe and first described by Fuente del Campo,
is one I have used for years and can recommend for
specific cases. This procedure uses a frontonasal
disjunction, as performed in a Lefort III. The nasal
lining is transected and lengthened with the insertion of an interpositional bone graft at the bony
frontonasal osteotomy. This allows major lengthening of the congenitally or traumatically shortened nose.
Figure 4-188 The exposure is obtained through a
coronal scalp incision and a transoral incision. A reciprocating saw is used to perform a frontonasal disjunction, exactly as is performed for a Lefort III procedure. Care must be taken to ensure that the level of the
cribriform plate is identified on the AP roentenogram
or CT or both. The osteotomy must be performed below

this level by a few millimeters to prevent any disruption of the plate or tearing of the dura. The saw cut is
then carried inferiorly and posteriorly to the pyriform
aperture. In patients with thick nasal bones, the osteotomy is carried in front of the nasolacrimal system,
as shown in this figure. The cut is most frequently extended posterior to the nasolacrimal system, as shown
in Figure 4-109, for correction of hypertelorism. The
same osteotomy is brought through the inferior wall of
the pyriform aperture. The nasal lining can be transected and a bone graft inserted with no negative effects. In order to significantly lengthen the nose, the
nasal lining must be transected. A length of ⱖ1–2 cm is
not uncommon.
Figure 4-189 After elongation, fixation with miniplates at the frontonasal region is performed by using
an interpositional bone graft.

ORBITAL SURGERY, HYPERTELORISM, AND NASAL RECONSTRUCTION

osteotome

Figure 4-188

Figure 4-189

CHAPTER 4

■ NASAL RECONSTRUCTION BY
USING THE FOREHEAD-FLAP
TECHNIQUE
A midline forehead flap based on the right or left
supratrochlear vessels is the best source for replacement of the skin for partial or total nasal reconstruction. I recommend including the frontalis
muscle in this flap to improve its vascularity. In
heminasal reconstruction, the flap is designed
obliquely on the opposite side to facilitate rotation
and minimize kinking of the pedicle. The skin flap
is transposed after reconstruction of the bony and
cartilaginous framework. Transection of the skin
pedicle and refinements of nasal reconstruction
are performed 3 to 4 weeks after the initial surgery.
I have used many of the refinements recently described by Burget and Minick.
Figure 4-190 The design of the forehead flap depends
on the height of the forehead and size of the nose. An
exact pattern and careful planning of a thin flap yields
optimal results. The forehead flap is transferred for
coverage after a turn-down flap from the left side of the
nose is used for lining on the right side.
Figure 4-191 The trifurcated design of the flap used
to reconstitute the columella and alar bases. Septal car-

tilage was used to reconstruct the lower lateral cartilages and create nasal-tip projection. The cartilaginous
framework is covered with a forehead flap.

proboscis.

CHAPTER 4

■ EXORBITISM AND RELATED
DISPROPORTIONS OF THE BONY
ORBIT AND EYE
Syndromal craniosynostosis is frequently associated with exorbitism caused by a small bony orbit.
This also is seen in hyperthyroidism, when excessive secretion of the thyroid-stimulating hormone
results in enlargement of the adnexal structures in
and around the eye. Exorbitism can be difficult to
correct with bony orbital shifting. However, several other procedures may be used to correct the
congenital disproportion of the orbits and exorbitism. Moving or removing the lateral orbit offers
satisfactory results, particularly in malignant exophthalmos associated with hyperthyroid disease.
Figure 4-220 Patient with exorbitism. The proposed
osteotomy of the inferior lateral orbit extends through
the base of the orbit into the inferior orbital fissure.

Figure 4-223 The procedure for correction of exophthalmos in patients with hyperthyroid disease was developed and described by Guido Matton. I consider this
procedure to be one of the most effective. In this procedure, the inferior lateral orbital rim, including a portion
of the internal lateral orbital wall, is removed, allowing
more room for the globe. This procedure can be performed through a transconjunctival subciliary incision
or coronal approach.
Figure 4-224 Preoperative frontal view of a 37-yearold patient with moderate exorbitism of congenital origin.
Figure 4-225 Postoperative frontal view of the same
patient 1 year after the procedure presented in Figure
4-223.

Figure 4-221 The lateral orbital wall has been expanded laterally. The intraorbital bone was removed to
gain additional expansion.

Figure 4-226 Preoperative submental vertex view
shows marked exorbitism.

Figure 4-222 The osteotomy is extended along the inferior orbit. The lateral orbital wall is rotated and fixed
posteriorly with a miniplate.

Figure 4-227 Postoperative submental vertex view 1
year later. The global bony orbital disproportion has
been improved by using this technique.

■ LOWER ORBITAL ADVANCEMENT
VARIATION FOR CORRECTION OF

Another effective procedure I have used to correct
exorbitism is an osteotomy performed in the upper
portion of the Lefort III. This can be performed
with or without advancing the lower maxilla, depending on whether there is any problem with occlusion.
Figure 4-236 The design of the procedure is shown
with a frontonasal disjunction. The osteotomy extends
posteriorly to the nasolacrimal system along the floor of
the orbit to the inferior orbital fissure. The incision extends laterally through the lateral orbital wall in front
of the temporal fossa. The lower portion of the os-

teotomy extends below the inferior orbital nerve at the
level of the Lefort I osteotomy.
Figure 4-237 Mobilization of the bony complex. The
zygomatic arches are cut on both sides.
Figure 4-238 Advancement of the bony complex is

lateral orbits and zygomatic arch bone grafts are secured with wires or plates.
