H A P T E R

5
MAXILLARY
••••••••••••••••••••••

OSTEOTOMIES

Kenneth E. Salyer

MAXILLARY OSTEOTOMIES

This chapter concentrates on the various proven
techniques that I use from Lefort I, II, and III with
variations that include the malar bone and orbits,
as well as the total monobloc advancement. The
chapter on syndromal surgery describes how many
of these procedures are used together.
One of the most important concepts in maxillary osteotomies is securing an appropriately positioned maxilla in the growing child, as well as
after completion of growth. Achieving this early
improves subsequent functional facial balance
and aesthetics. The key to success in facial surgery
lies in the achievement of facial convexity with
appropriate projection of the cheek bones and display of upper teeth along with the correct vertical
and anterior positioning of the maxilla, which
contributes to occlusion, positioning of the soft
tissues of the lips, and facial balance.
The most frequently used procedure is the Lefort
I osteotomy. I use many variations of the Lefort I
procedure; the most important are included in this
chapter. One variation that merits attention is in the
patient with a cleft undergoing Lefort I, in which
case a two- or three-piece maxillary osteotomy may
be necessary to achieve optimal dental positioning
and facial aesthetics. My personal technique for
Lefort I, which has evolved over many years, offers
a very efficient surgical positioning of the maxilla
and, with experience, can be performed rapidly
with minimal blood loss and tissue trauma. I use the
Lefort II infrequently; it is outlined here primarily
for correction of Treacher Collins syndrome. In
such instances, facial advancement is at the Lefort I
or II level because of deficiencies or absence of the
malar and zygomatic arches.

209

The Lefort III procedure is the procedure of
choice in major craniofacial deformities involving
the midface. In the patient with syndromal craniosynostosis, this procedure should be performed
early, during the time the child is aged 5–8 years,
and then repeated if necessary after completion of
growth. As distraction osteogenesis is used more
frequently, it will allow minimally invasive techniques that can differentially move the maxilla at
all levels, producing new bone formation. This can
be repeated as necessary to maintain facial aesthetics and function.
The combined use of the Lefort III plus the Lefort
I is important in achieving occlusal and facial correction simultaneously in patients after eruption
of the secondary dentition. This procedure allows
the surgeon differentially to move the orbital malar
region from the more dentally dependent or occlusion dependent lower maxilla. With the introduction of internal rigid fixation, the Lefort III plus
Lefort I is now a very stable procedure, which I use
frequently. I believe that this procedure provides a
better, more definitive aesthetic result because it
allows differential advancement of the orbital–
malar region and the occlusion-dependent lower
maxilla.
These techniques are outlined in the following
chapter. The more extensive procedures combine
the intracranial approach shown with variations of
Lefort III or the monobloc advancement or both,
which simultaneously correct the forehead, orbits,
maxilla, and midface. When such procedures are
combined with mandibular osteotomies, then
complete alteration of the craniofacial skeleton
can be achieved in one operation.

210

CHAPTER 5

■ LEFORT I MAXILLARY
OSTEOTOMY
Figure 5-1 This design for the Lefort I osteotomy is
the one that I most frequently use. The upper level of
the cut is varied, depending on the paranasal deficiency and whether the surgeon plans to include the
zygomatic arch in the procedure. The osteotomy
should be 4–5 mm above the root of the first bicuspid.
Figure 5-2 The Lefort I maxillary osteotomy begins
with an incision in the vestibular mucosa 3–4 mm
above the upper gingiva by using the Colorado needle.
This incision allows a cuff of mucosa to be left, facilitating postoperative closure. After reaching the bone,
the soft tissue is undermined by using a periosteal elevator.

Figure 5-3 The nasal lining is dissected from the
bone at the level of the piriform aperture and along the
nasal floor. It is important to dissect the nasal mucosa
from the bony floor of the nose before the Lefort I osteotomy. Dissection of the soft tissue on the front of the
maxilla is carried to the maxillary tuberosity and behind it to the pterygoid plate. Avoid entering the buccal fat pad.
Figure 5-4 The nasal mucosal lining is protected
with a ribbon malleable retractor, which is inserted
posteriorly along the lateral nasal wall. By using a reciprocating saw, the surgeon cuts through the anterior
wall of the maxilla and maxillary tuberosity and continues through the posterior wall of the maxillary sinus. If the posterior wall is too deep for the length of
the saw, then this step is completed with a thin
Dautrey osteotome.

Figure 5-8 The key to a safe and bloodless Lefort I osteotomy is pterygomaxillary disjunction with an elevator. I use a curved periosteal elevator, as depicted in this
figure. Just above the left instrument, the palatine branch
of the internal maxillary artery is seen. Surgical manipulations in this region must be performed with extreme
care. When the pterygomaxillary disjunction is complete, the maxilla, held between the maxilla and thumb,
can be moved easily in all planes without obstruction.
Figure 5-9 The next step involves the use of a prefabricated dental splint, which is wired into the posi-

two-jaw, I frequently use an intermediate splint. This
splint stabilizes the maxilla into its new position.

bony fragment blocking its proper positioning is removed.
Figure 5-10 Four wires are initially used for fixation.
As the wires are tightened, the surgeon checks to see
that the occlusal plane is at the proper level.

Maxillary elongation, a frequently performed procedure in patients with clefts, is commonly performed in combination with advancement, as described in the previous section.

of the maxillary paranasal deficiency.

with interosseous wiring, final internal rigid fixation is

CHAPTER 5

■ LEFORT I MAXILLARY SETBACK
Maxillary setback is a rarely performed procedure
that must be executed with care. The posterior
movement is limited to bone thickness and the
pterygoid plate.
Figure 5-41 The bone is carefully removed with the
rongeur to allow a maxillary setback. An alternative
technique is the use of a burr to remove an adequate
amount of bone from the pterygoid plate and the maxillary tuberosity. This allows the maxilla to be set back
posteriorly.
Figure 5-42 After removing the necessary amount of
bone to allow the maxilla to be set back, titanium plates
and screws are used for internal rigid fixation. The position is determined by a prefabricated occlusal wafer.
Figure 5-43 Preoperative frontal view of a 25-year-old
patient with an excessively protruding maxilla, requir-

ing a maxillary setback in combination with mandibular advancement to correct facial balance and occlusion.
Figure 5-44 Postoperative frontal view 5 months after
Lefort I maxillary setback with bilateral sagittal split of
the mandibular ramus with advancement.
Figure 5-45 Preoperative left lateral view shows maxillary–mandibular disproportion reflected in the positioning of the upper and lower lips.
Figure 5-46 Postoperative left lateral view 5 months
after Lefort I maxillary setback and bilateral ascending
ramus sagittal-split mandibular advancement. The
maxillary osteotomy involved a 4-mm setback as well
as a 4-mm right-sided and 3-mm left-sided impaction to
correct the associated vertical excess.

■ LEFORT I OSTEOTOMY FOR
VERTICAL ASYMMETRIC

MANDIBULAR RAMUS OSTEOTOMY
AND SLIDING GENIOPLASTY
In cases of an asymmetric maxilla, a differential osteotomy, with lengthening or shortening of one
side, may be indicated in combination with
mandibular surgery. This approach is used in
hemifacial and craniofacial microsomia, as discussed in detail in chapters 6 and 7.
Figure 5-47 The designed osteotomy of the maxilla
and mandible allows lengthening of the maxilla on the
left side and shortening on the right side to level the occlusal plane. This procedure is performed in combination with a bilateral ascending mandibular ramus sagittal split and a sliding genioplasty to correct mandibular
and chin asymmetries.
Figure 5-48 The operation is completed. The maxilla
is lengthened on the left side with an interpositional
graft of hydroxyapatite and internal rigid fixation. The
left side of the maxilla was shortened. The occlusal
plane is leveled after a bilateral sagittal split of the ascending mandibular ramus. A splint is used to secure
the occlusion in proper alignment. Correction is completed with a horizontal mandibular osteotomy by
moving the chin point to the midline.

Figure 5-49 A panoramic roentenogram after surgery
shows internal rigid fixation of the maxilla and chin
with single fixation of the sagittal-split mandibular ramus. This surgery was performed in the patient shown
in Figure 50.
Figure 5-50 Preoperative frontal view of a 17-year-old
patient with facial asymmetry and vertical shortening
on the left side. The center of the chin is displaced to
the left.
Figure 5-51 Postoperative frontal view 2 years after
surgical correction. Lefort I maxillary rotation with a hydroxyapatite interpositional graft lengthened the maxilla on the left side by 8 mm. A 2-mm impaction was
performed on the right side of the maxilla. The horizontal mandibular osteotomy moved the center of the chin
4 mm toward the right side to place it in the midline.
Figure 5-52 Preoperative submental vertex view before correction of the facial mandibular asymmetry.
Figure 5-53 Postoperative submental vertex view after correction of the facial asymmetry.

■ MAXILLARY SEGMENTAL
OSTEOTOMY IN COMBINATION
WITH MANDIBULAR SEGMENTAL
OSTEOTOMY
SEGMENTAL UPPER- AND LOWERJAW OSTEOTOMIES
This procedure is performed in cases in which the
six front upper maxillary teeth protrude, straining
the upper lip and causing facial imbalance. In this
procedure, the secondary maxillary and mandibular bicuspids are extracted on both sides to allow
maxillary and mandibular osteotomies with repositioning of all segments.
Figure 5-54 The proposed osteotomies indicate the
areas of bone to be removed for the rotational setback of
the maxillary and mandibular segments into a proper
occlusal plane. The gingivae are dissected from the
bone in the area of the four teeth that have been extracted. The bone is removed slowly, with a powerdriven burr protecting the gingivomucosal attachment.
Figure 5-55 The bony segments are pedicled on the
gingivomucosal flaps. A small spatula osteotome is used
to perform an interdental osteotomy. The remaining osteotomies are made with a thin reciprocating saw to minimize bone loss. Once these segments move easily into
their new proper alignment, they are fixed with a splint.
Small 0.4-mm plates from the CMS set with 0.9-mm
screws are used; care is taken not to injure tooth roots.
Figure 5-56 Preoperative frontal view of a 26-year-old
patient with marked protrusion of the front teeth, resulting in their excessive show, and protrusion of the
upper lip.

Figure 5-57 Postoperative frontal view 4 years after
upper and lower anterior six segmental maxillary and
mandibular osteotomies. The maxillary segment has
been moved ⬃6–7 mm posteriorly and downward. This
procedure was performed simultaneous with a sliding
genioplasty.

Figure 5-58 Lateral view of the proposed osteotomies.
In some cases, before surgery, orthodontic treatment
may be used to improve the position of the teeth. Note
the large area where the bone and bicuspids will be removed in the maxilla and mandible. Also note the incision for the sliding genioplasty.

Figure 5-59 The rotated maxillary segment and the
mandibular segment that is moved upward and rotated
are secured with an occlusive wafer, plates, and
screws.

Figure 5-60 Preoperative left lateral view with protruding upper teeth and lips. Note the excessive show
of the teeth.

Figure 5-61 Postoperative left lateral view 3 years after upper and lower anterior six setback with sliding
genioplasty. There is improved facial balance and harmony.

■ LEFORT II MAXILLARY
OSTEOTOMY
The Lefort II osteotomy allows correction of the
frontonasal disproportion by changing the position of the maxilla. The procedure does not involve the malar bone. I have used the procedure
primarily in patients with Treacher Collins syndrome in which maxillary elongation posteriorly
is combined with anterior maxillary impaction.
Figure 5-62 The Lefort II maxillary osteotomy begins
with a frontonasal disjunction, which must be performed below the level of the cribriform plate. The osteotomy continues posteriorly to the nasal lacrimal
fossa along the medial orbital wall to the floor of the orbit, and then to the inferior orbital fissure. The pterygomaxillary disjunction completes the osteotomy from the
inferior orbital fissure to the pterygomaxillary region.
Figure 5-63 Lateral view. The osteotomy extends
through the frontonasal junction, behind the lacrimal
fossa, along the floor of the orbit and inferior orbital fissure, and through the pterygomaxillary junction.
Figure 5-64 The osteotomy is carried on behind the
medial canthal ligament without detaching it. The osteotomy is then extended down the medial orbital wall,
joining the osteotomy at the floor of the orbit to the inferior orbital fissure.

Figure 5-65 The osteotomy is carried across the frontonasal junction, below the level of the cribriform plate.
The reciprocating saw is moved from the medial orbital
wall on one side through the medial orbital wall of the
opposite side.
Figure 5-66 After the osteotomy, complete mobilization of the maxillary complex, and bone grafting, internal rigid fixation secures the position of the maxilla.
The mandible has also been moved by using an inverted-L osteotomy with interpositional bone grafting.
A wafer secures the proper occlusal plane.
Figure 5-67 Preoperative right lateral view of an 11year-old patient with Treacher Collins syndrome with
marked retrognathic microgenia and other characteristics of Treacher Collins syndrome.
Figure 5-68 Postoperative right lateral view 4 years
after Lefort II maxillary advancement with mandibular
advancement by using an inverted-L osteotomy and interpositional bone grafting (see chapter 7). The patient
also underwent a bone-grafting procedure to the nose
and malar region.

CHAPTER 5

■ LEFORT III MAXILLARY
OSTEOTOMY

Figure 5-69 Illustrated is a Crouzon or Apert skeletal
deformity with the proposed incision lines for a Lefort
III maxillary osteotomy. I prefer to use this procedure
for these reasons: (a) it is a one-piece operation, allowing adjustment of the orbits and the maxilla; (b) it may
be repeated if necessary as the child grows; (c) it may be
performed in combination with a Lefort I procedure after eruption of the secondary dentition; and (d) it does
not damage dental tooth structures in the growing facial skeleton. The osteotomy is carried down the lateral
orbital wall from the frontoorbital junction to the inferior orbital fissure, across the floor of the orbit to the
medial orbital wall extending behind the nasolacrimal
fossa, and finally across the frontonasal junction. The
zygomatic arch is cut to allow advancement of the maxillary–malar complex.
Figure 5-70 Through a bicoronal scalp incision, the
subperiosteal dissection is extended along the lateral
orbital wall. The dissection is carried along the inferior

orbital wall and over the zygomatic arch by using a Colorado needle. The bone is used as a guide to prevent the
frontal branch of the facial nerve from injury.
Figure 5-71 In the Lefort III operation, taking down
all the attachments of the masseter muscle along the zygomatic arch and removing the attachments of the zygomaticus major and minor muscles along the malar
bone and the infraorbital rim are of particular importance. If this task is not accomplished, there is a chance
of fracturing the lateral portion of the orbit or zygoma
as the surgeon attempts advancement.
Figure 5-72 After completion of the osteotomies, the
maxilla is downfractured. Two Rowe disimpaction
forceps are placed in each side of the nasal passage
and the mouth, and pressure is applied to accomplish
the downfracture. The maxilla is gradually mobilized.

Figure 5-73 By using digital pressure, the maxilla is
brought forward, extending the separation of the frontonasal junction with the maxilla rotated forward and
down. The osteotomy of the pterygomaxillary region
follows the downfracturing of the maxilla. The osteotomy is performed from above with a long curved
osteotome. The most difficult part of the procedure is
mobilizing the maxilla. The pterygomaxillary junction
must be separated slowly and carefully. Stretching the
soft tissue in this area can be accomplished with a rib
spreader placed at the frontonasal junction. Sometimes
a fingertip may be used gradually to stretch the soft tissues. Once this is accomplished, digital pressure may
be used to bring the maxilla forward and downward,
extending the separation of the frontonasal junction.
Figure 5-74 By using an osteotome, the surgeon separates the vomer and septum from above. As is shown
here, the two-pronged osteotome is used, approaching
through the frontonasal junction from above, moving
both inferiorly and posteriorly, and transecting the
vomer from the nasal septum, which allows maxillary
advancement. This is performed after frontonasal disimpaction and before complete mobilization is
achieved.
Figure 5-75 Fixation of the Lefort III bony segments is
achieved with interpositional bone graft in the zygomatic arch, at the frontonasal junction, and in the lateral orbit. The bony segments are fixated by using
Medicon’s CMS system. A three-bar plate is used at the
frontonasal junction. Laterally, curved orbital plates
are used. Note the long plate used from the base of the
skull laterally on the temporal bone and then continued down posteriorly below the malar complex and
along the front of the maxilla. The use of postoperative
intermaxillary fixation improves the stability of the
maxillary segments. In cases in which relapse is ex-

pected, intermaxillary fixation as mentioned or elastics
and face-mask protraction or both postoperatively may
be indicated. Leaving the occlusal splint for 6–7 weeks
postoperatively aids in keying the occlusion.

The following series is taken from a case study of a
patient with Crouzon’s syndrome who first was
seen with increased intracranial pressure causing

III procedure for a class III relation of the teeth
with an anterior open bite. The drawings show the
patient after completion of growth. The proposed
definitive correction includes repeated Lefort III
and Lefort I procedures and a sliding genioplasty.
Figure 5-84 The cranium was previously expanded.
The proposed operation includes Lefort III and Lefort I
procedures and a sliding genioplasty.

patient with Crouzon’s syndrome with decreased vision resulting from increased intracranial pressure.

causing decreasing vision and papilledema.

Figure 5-89 Same patient shown 10 years after primary surgery. This patient with Crouzon’s syndrome
developed further midface deficiency and exorbitism.
At this time, surgical correction is planned.
Figure 5-90 Through the coronal incision approach,
the temporal muscles are dissected and turned down.
The attachment of the pericranium, which has been

The pericranium will be used to cover the surgical

to the frontoorbital junction. The inset drawing

by malleable retractors.

Figure 5-92 Downfracturing, by using the Rowe disimpaction forceps, is performed bilaterally after completion of the osteotomy. This step initiates the osteotomy in the pterygomaxillary region and begins the
mobilization of the craniofacial disjunction. Complete
mobilization is achieved with continued dissection of
the soft tissues, mobilization, and complete separation
of the pterygomaxillary region. The periosteal elevator,
the surgeon’s index finger, and, at times, an osteotome
or reciprocating saw or both are used to perform these
procedures.
Figure 5-93 Rigid fixation of the Lefort III is performed before the Lefort I procedure. This step allows
adjustment of the midface and the orbits before setting
the final occlusion. I use a three-bar plate at the frontonasal junction, with interpositional bone grafts laterally and medially in the frontonasal junction, lateral orbits, and zygomatic arch. A curved plate extends across
the zygomatic arch. An additional plate extends from
the base of the skull down to the maxilla to fixate the

maxilla in its new position. The screws are not placed
into the lower part of the maxilla until the Lefort I procedure is completed. Once the Lefort III is completed
and stable, the Lefort I is easily performed with a reciprocating saw.
Figure 5-94 A malleable retractor is inserted in the
piriform aperture to protect the nasal lining, and a reciprocating saw is used to cut through the maxilla. The
proposed pterygomaxillary disjunction has already
been accomplished, facilitating the procedure. The
plate from the base of the skull is fixed to the maxilla
once the new occlusion is keyed.
Figure 5-95 Osteotomy of the chin is performed. The
mental nerve is protected as the reciprocating saw cuts
through the chin for a sliding genioplasty, which is
combined with maxillary advancement. All the osteotomies and mobilizations of the Lefort I and sliding
genioplasty are completed before fixation is done.

Figure 5-96 Once the maxilla is mobilized into the
new position, it is fixed into an occlusive wafer. The
maxilla is fixed to the mandible in a new position. The
sliding genioplasty is secured with a single titaniumplate fixation.
Figure 5-97 The procedure is completed with fixation
of the Lefort I maxillary advancement by using CMS titanium plates, two on each side, to ensure stability of
the maxilla.

CHAPTER 5

Figure 5-106 Patients with syndromal craniosynostosis operated on early in life frequently develop a bitemporal indentation. To correct this deformity, I use my
variation of the Lefort III procedure. It incorporates the
Lefort III osteotomy with a lateral orbital osteotomy, including a portion of the temporal bone, which is moved
forward and out, correcting the indentation. These
steps allow maxillary advancement and recontouring
of the temporal region. This procedure requires intracranial exposure. It facilitates secure fixation, corrects the bitemporal indentations, and proves to be a
much more stable procedure than the classic Lefort III.
It is the next step toward a complete monobloc (see
chapter 4). A variation is illustrated in the inset. It
shows the procedure originally used by Tessier, which
incorporates a concept of Z-plasty, in which the lateral
osteotomy advances and fixes in front of the remaining
frontal bone, acting as a bone-locking procedure. This
variation, which can be fixed with a single wire or
plate, provides a much more secure advancement than
that performed without it.
Figure 5-107 Advancement is accomplished with interpositional cranial bone grafts, which are obtained
from the inner table of the bifrontal craniotomy and
placed in the frontonasal region and lateral orbit after
advancement. The frontal advancement is accomplished by simply advancing the frontal bone without a
bandeau. This variation often gives an improved result
and has been described by Kawamoto.

patient with Pfeiffer’s syndrome.

H A P T E R

6
MANDIBULAR
••••••••••••••••••••••

OSTEOTOMIES
••••••••••••••••••••••

AND GENIOPLASTY

Kenneth E. Salyer

MANDIBULAR OSTEOTOMIES AND GENIOPLASTY

In craniofacial surgery, we have the ability differentially to move the upper and lower jaw.
Mandibular osteotomies allow changes in dental
occlusion and in the projection and shape of the
lower facial skeleton. An aesthetically attractive
lower jaw must have enough angularity and projection to be in harmony with the rest of the face. It
is important that the upper, middle, and lower
thirds of the face are balanced.
Balance in the lower third of the face is primarily dependent on the symmetric position of the
basilar bone of the mandible, its bigomial width,
angularity, and vertical and anterior position in relation to the rest of the face. The most important
single aesthetic unit of the lower third of the face
is the chin. It can be moved independently in three
dimensions by using a sliding genioplasty.

245

In this chapter, I discuss the basic techniques
that I use most frequently. Mandibular surgery can
be performed accurately and predictably intraorally by using modern instruments. The safe performance of these intraoral osteotomies depends
on knowledge of the anatomy and its visualization.
Dental models, panoramic and cephalometric radiographs, and, at times, a computed tomography
(CT) scan are helpful in the planning and performing of mandibular surgery. The position of the inferior alveolar nerve dictates the design of the osteotomies performed on the ascending ramus and
body of the mandible, as well as on the chin. Complications of these procedures are minimal.

246

CHAPTER 6

■ SAGITTAL-SPLIT MANDIBULAR
RAMUS OSTEOTOMY
The most important osteotomy of the mandible is
the sagittal-split procedure originally developed
by Trauner and Obwegeser. Many modifications of
this procedure have been described. The following
technique is one I have developed by using only a
reciprocating saw to perform the osteotomy. I have
found this procedure to be an excellent approach
to the sagittal-split osteotomy. The disadvantage of
all modifications is potential damage to the interior alveolar nerve.
Figure 6-1 The inferior alveolar nerve branches off
the mandibular branch of the fifth cranial nerve. The
trigeminal nerve exits the brain via the foramen ovale at
the base of the skull and then enters the mandible via
the mandibular foramen at the lingula. It runs through
the body of the mandible, exiting at the mental foramen
as the mental nerve. Panoramic and cephalometric radiographs, using the lingula and mental foramen as reference points, allow identification and visualization of
the course of the nerve in the mandible. This information is essential to the surgeon while performing the
sagittal-split osteotomy, the sliding genioplasty, and
other mandibular osteotomies.

Figure 6-2 A bite block is inserted on the side opposite where the surgery is performed. The surgeon stands
on the right side of the patient and performs both leftand right-sided osteotomies from that position. Identification of the anterior border of the ascending ramus is
made intraorally by palpation with the surgeon’s index
finger. The initial incision in the oral mucosa is made
lateral to the border by using a Colorado needle.
Figure 6-3 A bifurcated periosteal elevator is used
along the anterior border of the mandible, stripping the
periosteum of its muscle attachments up to the coronoid
process, which is not shown in the drawing. A right-angle dental instrument facilitates identification of the
mandibular notch, an important anatomic landmark.
Figure 6-4 A bifurcated retractor is inserted high on
the coronoid process and held by an assistant. By using
a periosteal elevator, the lateral and medial subperiosteal plane is identified over the ramus and along the
body of the mandible down to the angle. The J-shaped
periosteal elevator is used at the lower inferior border
of the mandible.

MANDIBULAR OSTEOTOMIES AND GENIOPLASTY

Figure 6-5 The lingula and the inferior alveolar nerve
are identified on the panoramic radiograph. Medially,
the periosteum is freed above the lingula and below the
sigmoid notch. Identifying the inferior alveolar nerve
in isolation is not necessary. A lighted ramus retractor
is inserted medially above the level of the lingula, resting on the posterior border of the ascending ramus.
With the retractor rotated and hooked behind the posterior border of the ramus, the medial ramus corticotomy is performed. With a reciprocal saw, the corticotomy is carried through the posterior border of the
ascending ramus of the mandible and along the inner
cortical surface below the sigmoid notch and above the
lingula and nerve.
Figure 6-6 The corticotomy is carried through the inner cortical table of the ascending ramus to its anterior
border above the nerve.
Figure 6-7 The medial ramus retractor may remain in
place or be removed when retracting the mucosa anteriorly as shown. With a reciprocal saw, the osteotomy

is carried from the previous corticotomy, splitting the
anterior border of the ascending ramus by placing the
blade of the saw along the inner surface of the outer
table. With retraction of the mucosa along the anterior
border of the mandible, the remaining portion of the osteotomy is performed.

Figure 6-8 The osteotomy is continued through the
ascending ramus and onto the body of the mandible.
The saw blade cuts through the inner surface of the
outer table. This osteotomy also can be performed by
separating only the ascending ramus without bringing
the cut anteriorly through the angle or the body of the
mandible.
Figure 6-9 The osteotomy is completed by sawing
through the inferior border of the mandible. The saw is
then brought through the outer table of the body of the
mandible at a level at which the mandible can be advanced, maintaining contact between the bony segments.

Figure 6-10 In cases in which the osteotomy is performed with a saw, the proximal and distal segments
are easily separated by inserting a thin osteotome and
rotating it to separate the two segments.

Figure 6-11 When the two segments split with resistance, the osteotomy is continued or a bone spreader is
used or both. The lighted suction is used to visualize
between the outer and inner tables as they are separated
to make sure the neurovascular bundle is in the medial
distal segment. Further dissection of the periosteum
and attached muscles is necessary in some cases to obtain proper separation of the bony segments.

■ FIXATION OF SAGITTAL-SPLIT
MANDIBULAR RAMUS OSTEOTOMY
I begin the fixation by placing a single twisted wire
between the proximal and distal segments to bring
them together in their new position. It is important
that the condyles maintain their positions in the
temporal fossae. A prefabricated wafer is fixed to
the teeth with fine orthodontic wires, and the upper and lower jaws are placed in temporary intermaxillary fixation.
Figure 6-12 To fix the bony segment, I currently use
titanium or cobalt–chromium (Vitallium) plates with a
right-angle drill and screwdriver for screw insertion.
The plate is bent to the contour of the mandible, following the shape of the template. Two holes are placed
in the proximal segments, and two are placed in the
distal segments. With a right-angle screwdriver, screws
are placed through the outer and inner cortex of the
bony segments.
Figure 6-13 Rigid fixation is completed bilaterally.
The plate is fixed along the upper border of the proximal and distal bony segments. After the screws are inserted, intermaxillary fixation is removed so that the

mouth can be opened to check for any blockage of the
movements. Elastics are used for postoperative intermaxillary fixation for 6 weeks. They are removed when
eating and exercising the temporomandibular joints
postoperatively.
Figure 6-14 Preoperative frontal view of a 20-year-old
patient with previous irradiation treatment for a
mandibular tumor, resulting in mandibular hypoplasia
and retrogenia.
Figure 6-15 Postoperative frontal view 2 months after
bilateral sagittal-split mandibular advancement with a
sliding genioplasty with vertical lengthening and cranial bone graft onlay to the mandible.
Figure 6-16 Preoperative right lateral view of patient
with previously irradiated mandible.
Figure 6-17 Postoperative right lateral view after bilateral sagittal-split osteotomy plus a sliding genioplasty and cranial onlay bone graft.

■ INVERTED-L OSTEOTOMY
MANDIBULAR ADVANCEMENT WITH
BONE GRAFTING AND INTERNAL
RIGID FIXATION
The inverted-L osteotomy is difficult to perform,
but it results in less injury to the inferior alveolar
nerve. The technique may be performed from an
intraoral approach or from an external approach
through a submandibular incision.
Figure 6-22 A horizontal osteotomy is made above
the level of the lingula and the inferior alveolar nerve,
continuing posteriorly past the foramen. A vertical osteotomy is performed from this point down through the

ramus to the inferior border of the mandible by using
either a right-angle saw or a reciprocal saw.
Figure 6-23 The mandible is advanced with an interpositional bone graft. It is fixed in its new position with
a titanium plate with screws. This procedure results in
a stable mandibular advancement, and I use it often in
patients who are at great risk for relapse, such as patients with Treacher Collins syndrome.

■ MANDIBULAR SETBACK:
SUBCONDYLAR VERTICAL
OSTEOTOMY
Figure 6-24 The vertical osteotomy extends from the
mandibular notch through the inferior border of the
mandible, posterior to the lingula and the inferior alveolar nerve. I use a reciprocating or oscillating saw to
perform this procedure.

Figure 6-25 The mandibular setback with overlapping of the distal segment over the proximal segment is
shown fixed with lag screws. This old, proven procedure is frequently used with both intermaxillary fixation and single-wire fixation of the segments.

■ BILATERAL SAGITTAL-SPLIT
MANDIBULAR RAMUS OSTEOTOMY
WITH MANDIBULAR SETBACK
Figure 6-26 The sagittal-split osteotomy of the ascending ramus for mandibular setback is performed
in the same way as described earlier for mandibular
advancement. The difference lies in the removal of an
appropriate segment of bone and the use of a crosshatched, tongue-in-groove technique for fixation of
the bony segments. This design may be altered if
there is any rotational movement of the proximal segment.
Figure 6-27 Fixation by using the tongue-in-groove
technique requires only a single screw. The plates and
screws are placed above the course of the inferior alveolar nerve.

■ DISTRACTION OSTEOGENESIS OF
THE MANDIBLE
This technique allows the accurate elongation of
the body of the mandible or lengthening of the ver-

tical ramus on one or both sides and also results in
unilateral or bilateral mandibular advancement.

SINGLE-PLANE ELONGATION OF
THE MANDIBULAR BODY

Figure 6-36 A single-plane, double-pin distraction
device is inserted for the treatment of hemifacial microsomia. The corticotomy or osteotomy is performed
by using a reciprocating saw once the pins and the device are inserted in place (see Figures 6-55 through 662.) I start the distraction 5–7 days after the insertion of
the device and continue it at the rate of 0.5 mm twice a
day up to 1.0 mm/day until the chin is in the midline.

Figure 6-40 Preoperative frontal view of an 8-year-old
patient with right hemifacial microsomia and microtia
with absence of the ascending ramus and condyle on
the right.

Figure 6-37 The device is left in place with no further
advancement for 5 weeks to allow healing of the new
bone during the period of consolidation.

Figure 6-42 Postoperative frontal view of patient 2
years after distraction of previously placed costochondral graft and after insertion of a dermal fat graft and ear
correction.

Figure 6-38 This cross-section view demonstrates the
technique of corticotomy in which the inner table and
its portion of bone are left intact. As the distraction is
performed, the patient experiences a pop or crack after
several days of expansion. Ortiz-Monasterio and
Molina prefer to use a corticotomy and single pins for
distraction, believing that the new mandible is formed
with a more normal contour.
Figure 6-39 This cross-section view demonstrates an
osteotomy technique that preserves the nerve and normal medullary portion of bone. Either technique works
well, in my experience.

Figure 6-41 Frontal view at age 14 years during distraction of the costochondral graft inserted 4 years ago
for reconstruction of the right hemimandible and
condyle.

Figure 6-43 Preoperative right lateral view of an 8year-old patient with hemifacial microsomia and microtia and absence of the condyle and ascending ramus
on the right side.
Figure 6-44 Right lateral view at age 14 years during
distraction of the costochondral graft inserted 4 years
ago for reconstruction of the right hemimandible and
condyle.
Figure 6-45 Postoperative right lateral view after distraction of the costochondral graft before the time of final ear reconstruction.

■ TECHNIQUE FOR INSERTION OF
EXTERNAL MULTIPLANE
DISTRACTION DEVICE
Figure 6-57 This technique allows a three-segment
movement with two osteotomies by using the multiplane distraction device. When inserting the pins, attention must be paid to avoid tooth-bearing areas.
Panoramic and lateral cephalometric roentenograms
are useful for optimal placement of the pins.
Figure 6-58 Three small stab wounds are made for insertion of the pins through the skin.

Figure 6-59 A percutaneous trocar is inserted through
the skin down to the bone and left in place to protect
the skin.
Figure 6-60 With the trocar collar protecting the skin,
drill holes are placed through the outer and inner tables
of the mandible. Standard threaded half pins are inserted into each drill hole by using a powered pin
driver.

■ TECHNIQUE FOR INSERTION OF
INTERNAL SINGLE-PLANE
DISTRACTION DEVICE
Figure 6-69 Through an intraoral incision, the
mandible is exposed, and the osteotomy site is determined. Three holes for placement of the screws are
drilled posterior to the osteotomy site, and four holes
are drilled anterior to it. After inserting the device intraorally, the plates are held in place as the right-angle
screwdriver fixes the 6-mm screws, securing the device.
Figure 6-70 One week after the insertion of the device, I start the distraction at a rate of 0.5 mm twice a

day with the unique screwdriver, which fits into the
sleeve of the intraoral distraction device. The patient’s
family distracts the mandible on a daily basis.

Figure 6-71 The distraction device is removed after a
period of 5 weeks. Better internal devices, which allow
multiple-plane expansion, will eliminate the use of external pins in the future.

CHAPTER 6

■ GENIOPLASTY
The chin plays an important role in facial balance.
Surgical change in its position anteriorly and vertically, as well as its contour and shape, can have a
major impact on the overall aesthetic balance of the
face. The following variations are those I most frequently use in performing a sliding genioplasty. The
exposure for this procedure is outlined in chapter 1.
Figure 6-72 The osteotomy for the sliding genioplasty
is designed parallel to the occlusal plane. This procedure should be carried out 4–5 mm below the mental
foramen to protect the nerves. This may not be possible
when the mental foramen is low. The osteotomy is carried posteriorly as far as possible on each side to eliminate a step-off or indentation when sliding the segment forward.
Figure 6-73 The chin has been advanced and fixation
achieved with interosseous wiring. I prefer to use three

wires for fixation, but I may vary this type of fixation
depending on the degree of advancement and stability
of the segment (see Figure 6-81).

Figure 6-74 The incision in the lower sulcus leaves a
cuff of muscle below the teeth along the lower border of
the alveolar ridge, facilitating two-layer closure. Dissection is carried on subperiosteally at the level at
which the osteotomy is performed. A pedicle of tissue
remains around each mental nerve to protect it from
avulsion. The lateral osteotomy is performed below the
pedicle in a tunnel.

Figure 6-75 After the osteotomy has been performed,
the chin segment is advanced and fixed with interosseous wires. The holes are drilled through the
outer table of the proximal segment and inner table of
the distal segment. A 2-layer closure is shown.

MANDIBULAR OSTEOTOMIES AND GENIOPLASTY

cuff of muscle

Figure 6-73

Figure 6-74

sutures

interosseous
fixation

Figure 6-75

Figure 6-76 The nerve, as shown here, is protected
with a pedicle of tissue, and the saw is placed underneath the nerve. To achieve satisfactory aesthetic results, the osteotomy must be extended posteriorly to
maintain appropriate width on each side in the advanced chin segment.
Figure 6-77 The osteotomy is completed with advancement of the chin and fixation with three wires.
Soft-tissue closure is performed in two layers, as previously described.

of hydroxyapatite. The buccal fat pads also were removed.

CHAPTER 6

VERTICAL LENGTHENING WITH
ADVANCEMENT OF THE CHIN
Figure 6-88 Vertical lengthening can be achieved
with an interpositional graft of bone or hydroxyapatite
at the time of the sliding genioplasty. I have used autogenous or demineralized bone. There is less resorption
with hydroxyapatite as an interpositional graft than
with bone. Use of internal rigid fixation is mandatory
when using these materials to assure a secure fixation.

Figure 6-89 Vertical lengthening without advancement of the chin is more stable and may be fixed with
interosseous wiring in most cases. The technique is the
same as outlined.

VERTICAL SHORTENING OF THE
CHIN
Figure 6-90 This is rarely indicated, and the surgeon
should be very careful in making a decision vertically
to shorten the chin because of residual soft-tissue redundancy. This technique shows removal of a segment
of bone wider anteriorly than posteriorly, or pie
shaped. Certain cases may call for the removal of an exact amount of bone that is not a pie-shaped configuration but parallel.
Figure 6-91 Fixation with two or three interosseous
wires after vertical shortening without advancement is
usually very stable.

patient with excessive vertical length of her chin.

height without advancement.

ASYMMETRIC GENIOPLASTY
Figure 6-96 The chin is deficient on the left with the
midline off to the right. An asymmetric osteotomy is
performed, and the chin is slid over to the patient’s left
and advanced anteriorly with removal of a segment of
bone on the left. The bone is used to elongate the left
side to balance the chin.
Figure 6-97 The sliding asymmetric genioplasty is
fixed with three interosseous wires plus bone graft from
the excess chin to lengthen the left side. Many variations on this technique include performing a two-tier
operation with two osteotomies in the chin and a 180⬚
rotation of the central segment to produce symmetry.
Figure 6-98 Preoperative frontal view of an 11-yearold patient with hemifacial microsomia producing facial asymmetry with mandibular–maxillary disproportion, asymmetric retrogenia, and retrognathic mandible.

H A P T E R

7
SYNDROMAL
••••••••••••••••••••••

SURGERY

Kenneth E. Salyer
Antonio Fuente del Campo

SYNDROMAL SURGERY

Syndromal surgery involves a combination of
procedures to correct abnormalities caused by various syndromes, including Apert’s and Saethre–
Chotzen, Carpenter’s, and Crouzon’s. In syndromal craniosynostosis, I operate at age 6 months,
performing total cranial-vault remodeling outlined in this chapter and in chapter 2. Many of
these patients, however, require a repeated expansion of the cranial vault. Older patients who have
not had surgery and many secondary cases require
cranial-vault and corrective facial procedures,
which include variations of the monobloc procedure. After cranial-vault expansion at age 6
months in patients with syndromal craniosynostosis, I follow with a Lefort III advancement at age
6–8 years and then repeated cranial-vault surgery
at any age, if increased intracranial pressure develops. Final surgical correction is performed af-

281

ter eruption of the secondary dentition and surgical preparation by orthodontic treatment. It may
include the Lefort III and Lefort I procedures, or
Lefort II, Lefort I, mandible osteotomy, and sliding
genioplasty or a combination of these. Simultaneous or separate intracranial corrections also may
be performed.
With the advent of distraction osteogenesis, it is
possible to stimulate new bone formation in deficient maxillas and mandibles, even around toothbearing areas before growth is complete.
The difficult craniofacial and hemifacial microsomia cases illustrated in this chapter may necessitate repeated operations to achieve optimal results. The new techniques, including distraction
osteogenesis, allow marked improvement in the
treatment of such severe syndromes as Treacher
Collins.

282

CHAPTER 7

■ TOTAL CRANIAL-VAULT
RECONSTRUCTION FOR
SYNDROMAL CRANIOSYNOSTOSIS
Figure 7-1 In syndromal craniosynostosis, total cranial-vault remodeling is performed at age 6 months.
The operation is shown in a patient with an open
fontanelle, typical of Apert’s syndrome. The cranial
vault is too high, too wide, and short in the anteroposterior (AP) direction. By remodeling the cranial vault, I
lower the vertical dimension, expand the anterior and
middle cranial fossae, and reduce the bitemporal
width. A supraorbital bandeau is extended posteriorly
to the bitemporal region. A segment of bone is removed
to lower the height of the cranial vault. The four segments of the cranial vault are removed around the fused
midline suture. When the suture is not present, the cranial vault can be removed as one piece, two pieces, or
four pieces, depending on which sutures are fused or
abnormal.

Figure 7-2 Lateral view of the osteotomies shows the
supraorbital bandeau extending into the temporal region. The vertical dimension will be shortened by removing the strip of bone above the bandeau. Bitemporal narrowing will be achieved with a lower temporal
osteotomy, which extends through the orbit. The cranial vault is removed in four segments because of the
open fontanelle.
Figure 7-3 This view demonstrates the cranial-base
osteotomy which passes through the sphenoid wing in
front of the cribriform plate and extends posteriorly to
the temporal fossae.
Figure 7-4 With the patient supine, the osteotomy extends posteriorly and inferiorly as far as possible.

■ MODIFIED MONOBLOC
ADVANCEMENT
Simultaneous advancement of the frontal bones,
orbit, and face, as described by Ortiz-Monasterio et
al., can lead to infection caused by opening the anterior cranial fossa to the nasal cavity. I modified
the monobloc-advancement procedure by providing a partition between the brain and nose, which
acts as a barrier to dead space and subsequent infection. I have used this procedure for the last 15
years.
The modified monobloc procedure was first suggested by Anderl et al. A thin plate of bone, including the cribriform plate and orbital roof, is left
intact to act as a barrier between the brain and
nose.
Figure 7-26 An intracranial bifrontal craniotomy is
performed with a separate bandeau from the forehead,
leaving the orbital roof intact and a segment of bone below the cribriform plate between the Lefort III facial advancement and the intracranial contents. The operation
includes the following optional procedures: a new bandeau, which is split from bone behind the coronal sutures; a Lefort I; bilateral sagittal split of the mandible;
and sliding genioplasty. This procedure provides the
benefit of simultaneous change in the aesthetics and
function of the forehead, orbits, maxilla, mandible, and
chin, and can be combined with simultaneous orthognathic surgery.

Figure 7-27 The frontoorbitomaxillary advancement
has been performed and fixed with interosseous wires
and titanium plates and screws. The upper face and
midface are secured with wires, plates, and screws before performing the Lefort I osteotomy, sagittal-split
mandibular osteotomy, and sliding genioplasty. Occlusion is secured by an acrylic wafer and elastic bands for
intermaxillary fixation.
Figure 7-28 Preoperative frontal view of a 13-year-old
patient with Crouzon’s syndrome. The patient has
brachycephaly, midface hypoplasia, and retrognathia.
Figure 7-29 Postoperative frontal view 2 months after
a craniectomy, frontoorbital advancement with Lefort
III, Lefort I, and sagittal split of the ascending ramus of
the mandible.
Figure 7-30 Preoperative right lateral view shows exorbitism with midface maxillary and forehead retrusion, producing an impression of a pseudoprognathic
mandible.
Figure 7-31 Postoperative right lateral view after a
modified monobloc procedure with mandibular osteotomies.

CHAPTER 7

■ HEMIFACIAL MICROSOMIA
This syndrome results in facial asymmetry with
various degrees of skeletal deficiency of the cranial
vault, orbit, maxilla, zygoma, and mandible.
Definitive surgical correction of this deformity is
performed after eruption of the secondary dentition. Preoperative orthodontic treatment provides
leveling and alignment of the maxillary and
mandibular teeth. Surgical correction of the maxilla and mandible may involve Lefort I, onlay bone
grafting, distraction osteogenesis, sagittal split of
the mandibular ramus, and sliding genioplasty.
Figure 7-32 The surgical plan involved Lefort I with
bone grafting for elongation on the right and shortening
on the left, bilateral sagittal split of the mandible with
leveling and advancement on the right, and sliding genioplasty with split cranial bone as right mandibular
onlay graft.
Figure 7-33 The procedure is completed with an onlay graft of the split cranial bone to the mandible and an
interpositional graft to the maxilla. Fixation is achieved
with interosseous wires and titanium plates and
screws.

Figure 7-34 Preoperative frontal view of a 22-year-old
patient with hemifacial microsomia and microtia. Multiple previous procedures for correction of the malocclusion and mandibular and maxillary asymmetry were
not successful. She was referred for treatment from another institution.
Figure 7-35 Postoperative frontal view 3 months after
Lefort I maxillary osteotomy with elongation on the
right side of 5 mm after interpositional bone grafting.
The onlay bone graft to the right side of the mandible
and a bilateral sagittal-split mandibular osteotomy
were performed to achieve facial symmetry.
Figure 7-36 Preoperative right lateral view of a 22year-old patient with hemifacial microsomia with deficient right side of the face and retrognathic microgenia
and microtia.
Figure 7-37 Postoperative right lateral view 3 months
after maxillary Lefort I osteotomy with interpositional
bone grafting on the right side and bilateral sagittalsplit mandibular advancement. An onlay graft was
placed on the mandible on the right side.

CHAPTER 7

■ GOLDENHAR’S SYNDROME
This syndrome produces varying degrees of hemifacial (temporal, orbital, maxillary, and mandibular) asymmetry. Epibulbar dermoids distort the

globe. In classes IIIb and IV, the ascending ramus
and condyle are missing.

COSTOCHONDRAL RIB GRAFT
RECONSTRUCTION OF THE
CONDYLE AND ASCENDING RAMUS
WITH SUBSEQUENT DISTRACTION
OSTEOGENESIS
The first stage of treatment involves obtaining a
costochondral rib graft and implanting it to replace
the missing ascending ramus and condyle. In the
next stage, distraction osteogenesis is used to increase the dimensions of the graft.

Figure 7-39 The fibrous band connecting the glenoid
fossa with a remnant of the ascending ramus is dissected. A bloodless dissection is made possible by using a periosteal elevator and the Colorado needle.

Figure 7-38 Through an intraoral approach, the residual ascending ramus is freed of its abnormal attachment by a fibrous band to the underdeveloped glenoid
fossa.

Figure 7-40 Insertion of the costochondral graft for reconstruction of the ascending ramus and condyle. Intermaxillary fixation is maintained for 6 weeks postoperatively by using an occlusal splint and drop wires.

At this point in her treatment, the patient had 20 mm of
elongation. The single-plane expansion was inadequate
after further growth, and it was determined that additional distraction should be instigated. The treatment
plan included distracting the mandible and maxilla by
using a multiplane device to provide simultaneous
three-dimensional correction of the maxilla and
mandible.
Figure 7-50 NORMED multiplane external distraction device with three sets of two pins is inserted in the
costochondral rib graft for use in reconstructing the

however, the lengthening was not adequate. The corticotomy at the Lefort I level on both sides provides the
possibility for simultaneous maxillary distraction. The
maxilla is leveled by using interdental elastics on the
upper and lower teeth.
Figure 7-51 Frontal view shows patient at age 17
years after multiplane distraction before the removal of
the distraction device. The chin point has been moved
into the midline, and the occlusal plane has been leveled. Note the marked improvement in facial symmetry
and harmony.
Figure 7-52 This illustration demonstrates midline
correction of the chin with three-dimensional shift of
the maxilla and mandible by using distraction osteogenesis for correction of hemifacial microsomia. This
method is more stable than osteotomies with bone
grafting.

DISTRACTION OSTEOGENESIS IN
SIMULTANEOUS CORRECTION OF
MAXILLARY AND MANDIBULAR
DEFORMITIES IN GOLDENHAR’S
SYNDROME
This case study demonstrates the use of distraction
osteogenesis for correction of the maxilla and
mandible in hemifacial microsomia due to Goldenhar’s syndrome.
Figure 7-53 The proposed corticotomy of the maxilla is shown in the left oblique and frontal views similar to Lefort I cuts, and two corticotomies in the
mandible are shown for the distraction osteogenesis
procedure. The frontal view indicates the proposed
shift of the midline of the chin, which is off to the left
side by 1 cm.
Figure 7-54 Left lateral oblique view shows the distraction device with two pins placed in the mandible at
three sites. Distraction between the proximal and intermediate and distal segments provides vertical and horizontal lengthening of the mandible. The maxilla is
lengthened at the same time by using bonded brackets
on the teeth with intermaxillary elastics. New bone is
formed at the corticotomy sites of the maxilla and
mandible at the rate of 1 mm/day. The front view shows
the result with leveling of the maxilla and shifting of
the mandible to the midline.
Figure 7-55 Frontal view shows multiplane distraction device in place to maintain the distracted maxilla
and mandible during the period of consolidation.

patient with Goldenhar’s syndrome.

■ COMPLEX CASE STUDY:
FRONTONASAL DYSPLASIA WITH
CRANIOFACIAL MICROSOMIA AND
HYPERTELORISM
Figure 7-77 Preoperative frontal view of a 6-year-old
patient with a secondary craniofacial deformity after
failed intracranial correction of frontonasal dysplasia,
craniofacial microsomia, and hypertelorism at another
institution. This patient demonstrates the advantage of
early surgical intervention to improve balance and harmony in the craniofacial skeleton before completion of
growth.

■ TREACHER COLLINS
I have found the procedures described in this section helpful in the correction of Treacher Collins
syndrome. Although improvement can be
achieved, I believe more work needs to be done to
develop better techniques in the correction of this
deformity.
The primary pathology lies in the skeletal and
soft-tissue deficiencies of the orbits, maxilla, and
mandible. Tessier clefts occur in regions 6, 7, and

8, with varying degrees of deformity. The zygomatic arch may be absent and, with an abnormally
shaped bony orbit, the lateral and inferior lateral
floor of the orbit may be absent, producing a severe
deformity of the eye with an antigomial slant. The
malpositioning of the mandible, as well as the maxilla and nasal bones, ultimately requires surgical
correction. The associated soft-tissue deficiencies
in these patients make reconstruction difficult.

TEMPORAL MYOSSEOUS FLAP IN
TREACHER COLLINS
RECONSTRUCTION
The following two illustrations demonstrate a pericranial temporalis muscle–bone flap for reconstructing the missing zygoma and inferior orbital region.
Many outstanding craniofacial surgeons suggest
keeping the periosteum attached to the bone when
performing this procedure to prevent resorption of
the bone used for reconstruction.
Figure 7-84 The design of a T-shaped cranial bone
graft with the pericranium based on the temporalis
muscle attached to the bone. A section of the temporalis muscle is rotated 90⬚, together with the attached

bone, and secured to the orbit and zygomatic arch. This
procedure is performed through an intracranial approach, as noted by the dotted lines.
Figure 7-85 The muscle has been rotated with the attached temporal bone to create a new zygomatic arch
and orbital rim. Bone from the donor site is split and
used to fill the T-shaped defect at the donor site. I find
it difficult to shape the bone by using this technique because of the limitations of the bone attached to the muscle. I prefer cranial bone grafts rather than the
myosseous flap based on the temporalis muscle.

CHAPTER 7

FUENTE DEL CAMPO CONSTRUCTION IN
TREACHER COLLINS SYNDROME
■

Antonio Fuente del Campo

This technique is designed to reduce the projection of the nasal dorsum, narrow the bridge, and
amplify the flat area of the cheeks. The procedure
creates the visual effect of a setback of the protruded middle third of the face, thus reducing the
discrepancy between midface projection and the
hypoplastic malar areas. Consequently the amount
of bone needed to achieve projection in the malar
regions is reduced.
Figure 7-100 This procedure is uniquely designed to
heighten and narrow the nasal bridge, which is too
wide and flat in patients with Treacher Collins syndrome. A two-tier osteotomy is performed to form a
new nasal pyramid or triangle. A portion of the old
nasal midline bone is removed, and the osteotomies are
designed and cut as shown.
Figure 7-101 The procedure, completed with nasal
infracturing, results in a new narrower and lower nasal
bridge and much better nasal contour. This procedure
also results in the reduction of the discrepancy between the projection of the midface and hypoplastic
malar areas.

after the described rhinoplasty procedure, correction
of the lower eyelid coloboma, and sliding genioplasty.

CHAPTER 7

■ DOWN SYNDROME CORRECTION
I correct the facial appearance of patients with
Down syndrome by using a number of minor procedures with soft-tissue and bone augmentation
techniques. In changing the facial appearance, I intend to remove the stigmata of Down syndrome,
particularly the mongoloid slanting of the eyes,
flattening of the nasal and cheek areas, and the abnormal distribution of fat in the eyelids and
cheeks.

Figure 7-114 I reposition the medial canthus by using
the Fuente del Campo approach (see chapter 1) demonstrated in the drawing (arrow) at 12 o’clock. The medial
canthus is identified, transected, and reattached to the
periosteum in a superior medial and posterior location

by suturing it with 4-0 nylon sutures. Patients with
Down syndrome may have prominent ears, as shown at
the 2 o’clock position. They may be set back with a
standard otoplasty procedure. Liposuction of the neck
to remove abnormal fatty deposits and produce a better
mandibular–neck angle is shown at the 4 o’clock position. The use of a dorsal bone graft for nose augmentation is demonstrated at the 6 o’clock position. I formerly used a costochondral graft or a cranial bone graft
for this purpose but now use demineralized bone grafts,
which have yielded highly satisfactory results. The 8
o’clock position shows an intraoral incision for the removal or transfer of the buccal fat pad. The cheek or
malar augmentation or both are shown in the 10 o’clock
position. Formerly I used cranial bone for this purpose.
Presently I prefer to use demineralized bone, which has
produced excellent results.

H A P T E R

8
SECONDARY
••••••••••••••••••••••

CORRECTION OF
••••••••••••••••••••••

POSTTRAUMATIC
••••••••••••••••••••••

DEFORMITIES
••••••••••••••••••••••

Kenneth E. Salyer

CORRECTION OF POSTTRAUMATIC DEFORMITIES

Reconstructive procedures in patients with traumatic craniofacial deformities should be performed as soon as possible after the injuries are
treated and stabilized. Manson, Gruss, and others
demonstrated that detailed, meticulous reconstruction of the craniofacial skeletal deformity by
using extensive internal rigid fixation, primary
bone grafting, and a careful reassembly of the displaced segments greatly improves results, even in
massive injuries.
Unfortunately, many patients do not receive
state-of-the-art primary care, and therefore, secondary reconstruction is necessary. The correction
of secondary posttraumatic craniofacial deformities is more difficult than primary correction because of the displaced position of the bony segments and scar contracture of the soft tissue.

325

Cranial-vault posttraumatic deformities may require intracranial correction by using the remaining calvarial bone, if available, for reconstruction.
In cases in which additional bone is needed, I use
demineralized bone from the Pacific Coast Tissue
Bank (Los Angeles, CA). One of the most frequent
posttraumatic findings is enophthalmos after
malar or nasoorbital fractures when inadequately
corrected. Late enophthalmos may require more
than one procedure to achieve optimal results.
Secondary deformities after maxillary fractures
are best corrected with Lefort I osteotomy. In cases
in which the mandible has been severely fractured, improved facial balance is achieved with
mandibular osteotomies or a sliding genioplasty or
both.

326

CHAPTER 8

■ SECONDARY CORRECTION OF
POSTTRAUMATIC DEFORMITY OF
THE CRANIAL VAULT
The following case studies demonstrate techniques that I frequently use in the reconstruction
of secondary posttraumatic cranial-vault deformities. Debridement should be performed as soon as
possible after the injury occurs. All bone segments
should be preserved.
The following patient is a 22-year-old man who
sustained a motor vehicle accident ⬃18 months
before the secondary reconstructive procedure.
The patient had excessive debridement after the
injury in which bone segments were discarded. He
underwent an emergency craniotomy by using a
hemicraniectomy technique at the time of primary
correction. This created a major cranial-vault defect.
Figure 8-1 The calvarial defect involves most of the
right side of the cranial vault. Through a bicoronal incision, the entire cranial vault is exposed. The normal
cranial bone is removed with a craniotomy and split on
the back table, as described in chapter 1. The segments
taken from the normal side are used to close the defect
on the right side. The ventricular peritoneal shunt is
left on an island of bone for protection.

Figure 8-2 View from above. The cranial vault is removed, except for the bone surrounding the shunt. The
bone is split and used for reconstruction. On the left,
the shunt remains in place, whereas the island of cranial bone is rotated posteriorly to fit the contour of the
reconstructed head.
Figure 8-3 The large defect on the right side of the
cranium is completely closed with bone segments harvested from the split cranial bone on the unaffected
side. Several titanium plates were used for rigid fixation of the bony segments. The use of extensive rigid
fixation is shown in chapter 3 in congenital cases of hydrocephalus.
Figure 8-4 Intraoperative view. The dura is exposed.
Note the island of bone around the shunt.
Figure 8-5 View from the left side shows the removal
of adjacent cranial bone with the shunt in place.
Figure 8-6 The cranial defect is closed by using split
cranial bone from the opposite side and stabilized with
internal rigid fixation.

CHAPTER 8

■ SECONDARY CORRECTION OF
POSTTRAUMATIC CRANIAL-VAULT,
ORBITAL, MAXILLARY, MALAR,
AND NASAL DEFORMITY
This case study demonstrates reconstruction of the
cranial vault, orbit, nose, malar region, and maxilla after a gunshot wound. This 38-year-old
woman sustained a self-inflicted gunshot wound,
resulting in loss of the eye, orbit, and cranial vault
on the left side. There was loss of nasal soft tissue
and bone, and loss and displacement of the maxilla and malar–zygomatic complex.
Figure 8-25 The injury resulted in loss of soft tissue
and bone on the left side of the head and face, including the frontal bone, orbital roof, displacement of the
malar bone, medial orbital wall, nasal bone, and left
maxilla. There is also displacement of the malar bone.
A staged reconstruction was planned.
Figure 8-26 The first stage involves an intracranial
approach. Split cranial bone is used to close the defect
of the cranial vault, orbital roof, superior orbital rim, orbital floor, and medial orbital wall. The displaced nasal
bone and malar–zygomatic arch are repositioned and
grafted.
Figure 8-27 The second-stage skeletal reconstruction
included additional bone grafting by using split calvarial bone to reconstruct the nose, the medial orbital wall,
and the maxilla. Split calvarial bone was used to con-

struct an eye socket. Additional soft-tissue surgery was
performed to close the oronasoocular fistula. A septal
mucosal flap was used to close the nasal defect. A left
medial canthopexy and split-thickness skin graft were
used to build the left orbital socket. In addition, the
nose was reconstructed with concha cartilage grafts
and a composite skin–cartilage graft. A full-thickness
skin graft is used on the left upper eyelid.
Figure 8-28 Preoperative frontal view of the same patient with a cranial–orbital deformity resulting from a
self-inflicted gunshot wound.
Figure 8-29 Preoperative submental vertex view
demonstrates the absence of the cranial vault and orbit.
Figure 8-30 Postoperative frontal view 6 years after
reconstruction and insertion of an orbital prosthesis.
Figure 8-31 Frontal view of the same patient 3 years
after reconstruction with the addition of an orbital
prosthesis and a prosthesis for providing upper- and
lower-lid lashes.
Figure 8-32 A prosthesis with lashes is used around
the eye to give better aesthetic results.

CHAPTER 8

■ INTRACRANIAL APPROACH BY
USING A FOUR-WALL OSTEOTOMY
FOR POSTTRAUMATIC
DISPLACEMENT OF THE ORBIT
Figure 8-33 When the orbital roof is displaced posttraumatically, an intracranial approach is indicated for
correction. The intracranial approach is through a unilateral or bilateral frontal craniotomy performed by the
neurosurgeon. The amount of bone removed from the
medial and supraorbital rims is determined by the distance the orbit has to be moved medially and upward to
correct the posttraumatic displacement of the eye and
orbit. The orbital-rim osteotomy is performed below
the hemibandeau. The lateral orbital osteotomy extends through the malar region along the front of the
maxilla below the inferior orbital nerve to the floor of
the pyriform aperture. The osteotomy within the bony
orbit is 2 cm from the rim, so that the four walls of the
orbit can be translocated with the eye, moving the entire complex upward and medially. The bone removed
from the medial and superior rims is used for bone
grafting inferiorly. An additional amount of bone can
be obtained from the calvarium.

Figure 8-34 Mobilization and fixation of the orbital
osteotomy is completed by placing bone grafts below
the floor of the orbit. It is necessary to make sure that
the level of the orbital floor on the affected side is identical to that on the opposite side to secure the proper
position of the globe. Fixation of the bony segments is
achieved with interosseous wiring or internal rigid fixation or both.

Figure 8-35 Preoperative three-dimensional CT
shows displacement of the left orbit with the orbital
roof displaced downward.

Figure 8-36 Postoperative three-dimensional CT 2
years after surgical four-wall osteotomy and bone grafting to reposition the left orbit.

■ CORRECTION OF POSTTRAUMATIC
ENOPHTHALMOS WITH MALAR
BONE DISPLACEMENT (TRIPOD
FRACTURE)
Enophthalmos with displacement of the malar
bone is the most frequent posttraumatic facial deformity in my practice. Of 150 cases, ⬎90% had associated skeletal displacement resulting in increased orbital volume and enophthalmos. By
using craniofacial surgical techniques, it is possible to reconstitute the volume of the orbit by repositioning the displaced bony orbit and adding bone
grafts to translocate the globe anteriorly. Enophthalmos is corrected through a subcranial coronal
approach when the orbital roof is not displaced.
An intraoral or subciliary incision aids in this procedure.
Figure 8-37 A secondary posttraumatic deformity after an inadequately reduced tripod fracture with displacement of the malar bone inferiorly and laterally.
This increases the orbital volume, resulting in displacement of the globe and enophthalmos.
Figure 8-38 Correction is performed through the
coronal and intraoral approach with exposure of the
zygomatic arch, maxilla, and orbit. A subciliary or
transconjunctival incision may be added if additional
exposure is needed. An osteotomy is performed along
the lateral orbit through or above the old fracture site,
including as much of the lateral orbital wall as possible.
It continues down to the inferior orbital fissure, across
the floor of the orbit, and down the front of the maxilla,
just lateral to the inferior orbital nerve. The osteotomy
is brought across the maxilla so that the displaced segment can be completely mobilized. This recreates the
tripod fracture so that the displaced orbit can be easily
repositioned and secured by using the Medicon CMS
(Tuttlingen, Germany) system for internal rigid fixation. After stabilization, additional demineralized perforated bone grafts are placed as necessary to correct
the position of the globe. I use soft, malleable 1- to 3mm thick lamellar-type demineralized bone because of
the ease it provides in achieving the desired shape. I

place as much bone as necessary into the orbit, overcorrecting the anterior projection of the eye by 10–20%.
Figure 8-39 Cross-sectional view of the enophthalmos. The eye is sunken in the socket because of displacement of the malar bone and increased orbital volume.
Figure 8-40 The orbital volume is adjusted once the
displaced fractures are repositioned by using osteotomies and internal rigid fixation. I prefer using
lamellar demineralized perforated bone as orbital implants for correction of enophthalmos.
Figure 8-41 Cross-sectional view of the grafts placed
in all four quadrants of the orbit. It is critical to place
the grafts behind the equator of the globe so that the eye
is brought forward, correcting the enophthalmos. I usually overcorrect by 10–20% to allow soft-tissue contracture and changes due to bone-graft remodeling.
Figure 8-42 Preoperative frontal view of a 37-year-old
patient after facial trauma sustained from a blow to the
face in a horseback-riding accident. She was seen for
secondary correction of enophthalmos of the right eye,
displacement of the lateral canthus, hollowness of the
upper lid, and a palpebral fold due to increased orbital
volume.
Figure 8-43 Postoperative frontal view 3 months after
a C-osteotomy of the orbit with malar and intraorbital
demineralized bone grafting. The soft-tissue correction
included a lateral canthopexy and a right subperiosteal
elevation of the soft tissues of the face.
Figure 8-44 Preoperative right lateral view of patient
shows displacement of the cheek and enophthalmos.
Figure 8-45 Postoperative right lateral view 3 months
after correction.

CHAPTER 8

■ SECONDARY CORRECTION OF
POSTTRAUMATIC CRANIOFACIAL
DISJUNCTION WITH LEFORT III AND
NASO–ORBITAL–ETHMOID
FRACTURE AND MULTIPLE
MANDIBULAR FRACTURES
Maxillary and mandibular fractures, when not
treated appropriately, may lead to abnormal position of the bony segments and distortion of the facial appearance and proportions. Frequently, vertical posttraumatic shortening of the maxilla
occurs with retrusion, usually as a result of an inadequately treated Lefort III craniofacial disjunction. Proper secondary treatment requires maxillary advancement in addition to reconstruction of
the naso–orbital–ethmoid complex. The mandibular deformity also requires osteotomies and repositioning of the segments.
This patient exemplifies secondary correction
after severe facial fractures. The trauma was
caused by a tire-rim explosion that produced a
Lefort III craniofacial disjunction with a naso–orbital–ethmoid fracture and multiple mandibular
fractures.

this case, it will be used for nasal bone grafting and additional buttressing of bone needed in the procedure.

performed.

This patient demonstrates an extensive corrective

front of the maxilla initially led to good results.

change in appearance, residual deformity, and displacement of the bony segments.
Secondary reconstruction included removal of
all primary plates and screws, a craniotomy, and
the following staged corrections.

H A P T E R

9
CRANIOFACIAL
••••••••••••••••••••••

TUMORS

Kenneth E. Salyer
Stephen P. Beals
Edward F. Joganic
David A. Hidalgo

CRANIOFACIAL TUMORS

New craniofacial techniques have allowed successful cranial-based surgery and provide a
facial–cranial approach to major intracranial
tumors. By using these techniques, multiple segments of the craniofacial skeleton and soft tissue
may be disassembled to expose deep-seated tumors. This approach enables the neurosurgeon to
resect these tumors more efficiently under direct
vision and magnification. Various parts of reconstructive procedure may be performed on the back
table to speed up the operation. Transfacial approaches, described by Beals and Joganic, are useful as an overall framework for the disassembly of
the face for exposure of the cranial base.
Tumors treated with the combined craniofacial
approach include massive meningioma, fibrous
dysplasia, neurofibromatosis, cystic hygroma, hemangiomas, and others.
A massive meningioma involving the intracranial and facial regions required an intracranial approach, as well as a subcranial facial approach,

349

and is presented in this chapter in detail. Fibrous
dysplasia in the frontoorbital region requires a
combination of the intracranial and subcranial approaches. I favor complete resection of these tumors with immediate reconstruction by using split
cranial bone or demineralized bone. Neurofibromatosis requires aggressive surgical resection,
which unfortunately must usually be repeated
over time.
Hemangiomas present a difficult problem for
surgical treatment. In large cavernous hemangiomas, I prefer to wait for involution of these tumors, which occurs in the majority of these cases.

I INTRACRANIAL TUMORS
Stephen Beals and Edward Joganic have organized
the transfacial exposure to the anterior cranial
fossa and clival tumors. The following section outlines this approach.

CHAPTER 9

I FIBROUS DYSPLASIA
I have treated ⬎50 cases of fibrous dysplasia in the
craniofacial region, frequently in the frontoorbital
area. The monoosteitic type is regional but may be
extensive.
I prefer extensively to resect the tumor and reconstruct the bony defect with split cranial bone.
Recurrent growth requires further resection; however, in my practice, I have not had to repeat the
procedure. We have had no malignancies in our series to date.
Figure 9-41 Fibrous dysplasia in a 23-year-old female
involving the right orbital roof, frontal, parietal, and
temporal bones, sphenoid wing, and base of the skull
on the right side with minimal involvement on the left.
Figure 9-42 Through a bifrontal craniotomy with a
combined simultaneous resection intracranially and
orbitally, the patient’s right orbit down to the inferior
orbital fissure is resected. The sphenoid wing, a portion
of the temporal bone, and the middle temporal fossa are
resected. The frontal sinus affected on both sides is
completely resected. Note the split cranial bone prepared for reconstruction.
Figure 9-43 Reconstruction of large postoperative defects is completed by using split cranial bone from the
normal side. The orbital roof, lateral orbital wall, temporal bone, and supraorbital rim are fixed by using internal rigid fixation. The position of the eye is adjusted
with additional bone implants inside the orbit. After reconstruction of the bony skeleton, the temporalis muscle with pericranium is reattached over the reconstructed area.

CHAPTER 9

I NEUROFIBROMATOSIS
Neurofibromatosis is a perplexing problem of an
expanding lesion that infiltrates all tissue planes
and cannot be totally removed. I have developed a
more aggressive surgical approach to these lesions
as I have treated them through the years. The surgical techniques used in the two cases outlined
here are examples of techniques used for treating
this difficult problem. The combined approach of
the pediatric neurosurgeon and craniofacial surgeon offers the best possibility for superior results.

I have treated several patients with massive cystic hygroma of the head and neck which involved
all tissue planes and structures and produced progressive lymphatic obstruction. When the base of
the tongue, trachea, and neck are involved, the
growth can be fatal, even with repeated surgical resection. I am in favor of early aggressive excision
and, when necessary, I do not hesitate to sacrifice
the facial nerve.

I HEMANGIOMA
I delay surgical treatment of capillary hemangiomas, expecting involution of the tumor, which
occurs in the majority of cases. Only when the tumor causes visual obstruction and potential amblyopia of the eye or other problems, such as
bleeding, do I consider primary excision.

Figure 9-105 Frontal view of a 10-month-old patient
with an extensive capillary cavernous mixed-type hemangioma. Previous treatment included irradiation
and steroid injections, but there was no response. I decided to treat the patient with a continuous-pressure
mask to accelerate the involution of the tumor.

Figure 9-97 Frontal view of a 3-month-old patient
with early capillary hemangioma in the glabellar region.

Figure 9-106 Frontal view of same patient after 8
months of mask therapy with evidence of involution.

Figure 9-98 Two months later, considerable growth
of the lesion occurred. The lesion was bleeding and
breaking down. I decided to wait for involution to occur.
