CHAPTER 9

FIBULAR FREE-FLAP RECONSTRUCTION OF THE MANDIBLE
AFTER MALIGNANT TUMOR RESECTION
I

David Hidalgo

David Hidalgo’s technique is, in my opinion, stateof-the-art in reconstruction of the mandible by using a fibular free flap after tumor excision. Healing
problems are minimal with the use of well-vascularized tissue. To assess accurate mandibular reconstruction and bone remodeling, it is necessary
to use a lateral cephalogram and transverse-plane
CT scan of the mandible.
Figure 9-113 The mandibular contour is marked on
the lateral cephalogram.
Figure 9-114 The contour in the transverse plane is
marked on the CT scan image, reproduced on a 1 : 1
scale. Templates of flexible clear acrylic are cut out
from these tracings for intraoperative use.
Figure 9-115 Mandibular resection and the procurement of the fibular graft are performed simultaneously
by two teams. One team resects the mandible, while the

other obtains the graft from the donor site. An incision
in the lateral aspect of the leg is made and includes a
skin-island flap. The dissection is carried down for exposure and isolation of appropriate vessels. The donor
fibula with attached soft tissue is removed along with
the flexor hallucis longus muscle. This muscle is useful
for filling in the soft-tissue defect in the submental and
submandibular triangles and contributes to establishing a normal neck contour.
Figure 9-116 Cross-section through the midcalf
shows the fibula and attached soft tissue and skin island used for reconstruction.
Figure 9-117 The sequence of steps in mandibular reconstruction includes creating the appropriate shape of
the fibular implant, inserting the bony portion to replace the removed portion of the mandible, revascularizing of the flap, and completing soft-tissue closure.

Figure 9-118 I avoid incisions that split the lip if the
mandibular tumor can be significantly exposed for resection. The T-extension for the radical neck dissection
must be carefully planned.
Figure 9-119 The templates, based on the lateral
cephalogram and 1 : 1 ratio CAT scan, accurately determine the graft shape in two planes. The removed portion of the mandible provides precise measurements as
well as additional reference for the configuration of the
bone and soft-tissue components of the defect. Hemimandibular defects begin with the planning and location of the vascular pedicle so that it comes to lie at the
angle of the mandible. The portion of the bone graft corresponding to the angle is therefore shaped first.
Figure 9-120 The bone graft is inserted and fixed by
using internal rigid fixation with titanium plates and
screws. Intermaxillary fixation is used at the time of
surgery to maintain proper occlusion and to make sure
the fibular implant fits precisely in the removed
mandibular segment. Soft tissues are sutured into
place, and a skin island serves as a monitor for postoperative anastomosis.

RECONSTRUCTION

Kenneth E. Salyer
Jurgen Holle
Bernard S. Alpert
Satoru Nagata

MICROSURGERY, MICROTIA, AND SOFT-TISSUE RECONSTRUCTION

In this chapter, I present a few soft-tissue refinements that complement and enhance craniofacial
surgery. Microsurgical transfer of bone or soft tissue or both provides many possibilities for craniofacial reconstruction. The concept of providing
immediately vascularized tissue, which has been
shaped and formed or prefabricated and prevascularized before transfer, offers many new possibilities. I have included a very innovative example of
prefabrication by using the scapular flap, which
provides improved functional and aesthetic results, as described by Jurgen Holle. His design uses
precise planning with three-dimensional (3-D)
computed tomography (CT) to provide simultaneous mucosal coverage of osseointegrated dental
implants and simultaneous one-piece alveolarridge reconstruction of the hard palate.
Bernard Alpert’s free-flap technique with the
serratus anterior as a static contour filler in facial
reconstruction or as a dynamic muscle transplant

391

provides aesthetic and functional refinements. It is
particularly helpful in severe hemifacial microsomia cases after proper skeletal reconstruction or in
hemifacial atrophy as in Romberg’s disease or
other related soft-tissue deficiencies of the face.
Soft-tissue flaps with temporalis muscle and
pericranial flaps provide many possibilities and
variations for closing defects of the cranial base
and cranial vault. A temporalis muscle flap with
attached pericranium is useful when coverage is
needed in condylar joint reconstruction. Reconstruction of the external ear for microtia is presented according to the technique developed by
Satoru Nagata. His personal dedication to detail
and refinement serves as a good example of the use
of artistry in achieving superior results in this very
highly specialized area. This is true for all craniofacial and cleft surgery, as presented in these two
volumes.

392

CHAPTER 10

MICROSURGICAL RECONSTRUCTION OF THE ALVEOLAR
RIDGE WITH A SCAPULAR FLAP
I

Jürgen Holle
Alveolar ridge and hard-palate reconstruction has
functional and aesthetic aims that include the use
of osseointegrated dental implants. Inadequate
oral and nasal mucosa lining frequently make their
use impossible.
I have selected the lateral border of the scapula
as the optimal donor site because it simulates the
shape of the alveolar ridge of the maxilla and the
adjacent hard palate. I believe that inserting the
dental implants before transfer decreases the risk
of infection and loss of the implants. To achieve an
optimal soft-tissue lining similar to the oral mucosa with its attachment to bone, the following
procedure has been successfully developed.
An appropriate part of the lateral border of the
scapula is prefabricated by using precise planning
with 3-D CT. The lateral border of the scapula is
vascularized by the circumflex scapular artery and
vein and has an acceptable long vascular pedicle.

Figure 10-1 An appropriate segment of the lateral
border of the scapula is selected. Only a small layer of
musculature remains attached to the border that is osteotomized, taking care to include the nutrient branch
of the vascular bundle.
Figure 10-2 The osteotomized bone is pedicled on the
vascular branch of the circumflex scapular vessels supported with computerized placement of dental implants. It is covered with an oral mucosa graft or splitthickness skin graft, and to prevent ingrowth of
connective tissue, it is covered with a thin Gortex sheet.
The prefabricated flap is left in place between the musculature of the shoulder for 3 months to allow time for
osseointegration.
Figure 10-3 Three months later, the bony scapular
flap with the dental implants is harvested, together
with its vascular bundle. The previously inserted Gortex wrap facilitates the dissection and is removed before transfer to the face.

donor artery and vein are anastomosed to the facial vessels.
Figure 10-5 The donor artery and vein are joined to

grafting.

CHAPTER 10

FREE SERRATUS ANTERIOR STATIC AND DYNAMIC MUSCLE
TRANSPLANTATION
I

Bernard Alpert
The serratus anterior is a large muscle arising from
the first to ninth ribs. The lower three slips arising
from the seventh through ninth ribs lend themselves to transplantation because of their accessibility and relatively independent nerve and blood
supply. The muscle provides a block of tissue as a
vascular filler for a variety of defects by using one,
two, or three slips, depending on the size of the defect. The three lowest slips of serratus also provide
an excellent dynamic transplant for use in facial
paralysis. Its use here is demonstrated for contour
reconstruction and is particularly applicable in reconstruction of the soft-tissue defect in hemifacial
microsomia and hemifacial atrophy.
The slip arrangement of serratus is useful because of the ability to fill serpentine and irregular
defects. One must develop the ability to estimate
whether muscle fill is the procedure of choice, as
there will be ⬃30–40% atrophy.
Figure 10-14 The exposure of muscle is similar to the
initial dissection of the latissimus dorsi except that the
incision is considerably smaller and parallels the
eighth rib at about the anterior-axillary line, curving
posteriorly and superiorly to the end of the axilla. Key
landmarks are the same as for the latissimus dissection.
Figure 10-15 The underlying anterior border of the
latissimus muscle is identified under the lower portion
of the latissimus, serratus, and external oblique muscles, which interdigitate and must be separated
sharply. The major blood supply to the lower three
slips comes from continuation of the subscapular–thoracodorsal vascular axis, which is identified once the

latissimus muscle is elevated. This can be visualized
on the undersurface of the latissimus or lying on the
serratus muscle as these vessels come out of the axilla.
They are usually covered by a fatty areolar tissue and
must be carefully separated from the surrounding multiple branches that enter the adjacent muscles.
Figure 10-16 The interval between slips seven and
six can be developed with the tip of the finger on the
undersurface of the muscle. Once this interval cleavage
plane has been established, the direction can proceed
from the chest wall to the angle of the scapula, separating the lower three slips completely. Care must be
taken in approaching the neurovascular pedicle. Every
effort should be made to preserve the nerve supply to
the higher slips. This is usually easy to do because a
definite branch of the nerve slips off to supply slips
seven through nine. The vascular pedicle can be traced
as far as necessary all the way to the axillary artery.
Slips seven through nine are now detached from their
costal origin. They interdigitate with the internal
oblique muscle and must be separated sharply. The serratus is now isolated entirely on its neurovascular pedicle and is prepared for transfer.
Figure 10-17 The facial defect is shown, and the serratus has now been transected with its long arterial
pedicle and short nerve pedicle ready for anastomosis.
The facial artery and vein are preferable as recipient
vessels. The superficial temporal artery and vein may
be used as back-up for other neck vessels; for example,
the superior thyroid artery or any neck vein except the
external jugular vein. All anastomosis should be end to
end, if possible. Occasionally end to side is needed if
the external carotid artery or an internal jugular vein (or
both) are used.

I DESIGN OF THE ABBE FLAP
Figure 10-34 Removal of scar tissue in the lip is performed. The scarred lip tissue is excised with as much
normal soft tissue maintained as possible, and identification of the two peaks of the Cupid’s bow for future suturing of the Abbe flap. The lip is lengthened by incisions below the nasal floor in an attempt to bring the lip
down. The design of the length of the Abbe flap is the
length of the future upper lip and not the length as measured before release.

Figure 10-35 The Abbe flap has been dissected, and
one marginal vessel, on the left side, is identified and
maintained. The dissection incorporates identification of the marginal vessel on the right, which is ultimately transected, and then a very small soft-tissue
pedicle is maintained around the marginal vessel on
the left side.

Figure 10-36 The Abbe flap is rotated and inset, fea-

turing mucosa, muscle, and dermis of the lip and donor
site.
Figure 10-37 Ten days later, the pedicle flap is transected and inset.

the use of an Abbe flap and nasal reconstruction with

CHAPTER 10

I SOFT-TISSUE RECONSTRUCTION
PERICRANIAL FLAP
One of the most versatile tissues used in craniofacial surgery is the pericranial–periosteal flap. A
pericranial flap can be based on the temporalis
muscle, which allows an extensive radius of coverage including the entire cranial base, frontal sinus, and the entire cranial vault. The design of the
flap is individualized. It is used to close communication between the brain and face and to seal off
the frontal sinus and when there is anterior cranial-vault dead space.
Figure 10-41 My primary design for a pericranial flap
is shown. The flap is based on the temporalis muscle.
The temporalis muscle posteriorly is back-cut to allow
rotation, transposition, or advancement.

isolation of the cranial fossa from the nasal cavity, as
well as isolation of the nasal cavity from the oral cavity.

Figure 10-44 The design of bilateral temporal pericranial flaps. Each extension of the attached pericranium flaps is juxtaposed, allowing extension of the
flaps on both sides. One flap is designed anteriorly past
the midline, and the other leaves the pericranium posteriorly, attached to the temporalis muscle.

Figure 10-45 The use of the extended pericranial temporalis muscle flaps for closure of the cranial base.
These pericranial temporalis muscle flaps can be used
in any location in the cranial base to close off the communication of the cranial vault with adjacent facial and
oral cavities.

Figure 10-42 This illustration demonstrates the pericranial flap based on the temporalis muscle on the left
side. It extends over and across the midline to the opposite temporalis muscle. I use a similar design to seal
off the frontal sinus from the anterior cranial-vault
fossa when the sinus is open or exposed.

Figure 10-46 Preoperative frontal view with intraoral
presentation of a palatal encephalocele. This patient
has the typical facies with flattening of the frontonasal
region.

Figure 10-43 A rare but complex case is an encephalocele herniating in the posterior nasal pharynx
or into the palate. The encephalocele may extend
through the palate and be seen intraorally, requiring

Figure 10-47 The same patient 9 years after surgical
removal of the encephalocele and closure of the defect
in the cranial base. The pituitary gland is typically involved, requiring treatment by an endocrinologist.

CHAPTER 10

I TEMPORALIS MUSCLE TRANSFER
IN TEMPOROMANDIBULAR-JOINT
RECONSTRUCTION
The pericranial temporalis muscle flap can be
used to wrap the temporomandibular joint, providing a smooth articular surface after the reconstructive procedure.
Figure 10-48 I use a pericranial temporalis muscle
flap in reconstruction of the temporomandibular joint
when (a) the joint is present and interposition of tissue
is needed to prevent the costochondral graft from adhering to the raw surface of the bone; and (b) in temporomandibular joint ankylosis to interpose soft tissue
to prevent bone-to-bone healing and prevent recurrence of ankylosis (see chapter 7). This case of arthrogryposis of the temporomandibular joint with degeneration of the condyle and articular cartilage and limited
range of motion demonstrates the use of bilateral temporomandibular joint arthroplasty with an interposition of the temporalis muscle flaps. Intraoperative findings showed severe degeneration of the condyle,
meniscus, and fossa. The condylar heads were excised,

and bilateral pericranial temporalis muscle flaps were
inserted to prevent ankylosis.
Figure 10-49 Preoperative panoramic roentgenogram
demonstrates bilateral condylar degeneration in a 13year-old patient.
Figure 10-50 Intraoperative view demonstrates the
transposition flap of temporalis muscle and pericranial
flap into the space occupied by the resected condylar
head.
Figure 10-51 Preoperative frontal view at age 14 with
ankylosis due to condylar degeneration from arthrogryposis.
Figure 10-52 Postoperative lateral view of the same
patient with 25-mm opening after reconstruction.

CHAPTER 10

I DERMAL FAT GRAFT
The dermal fat graft, in my experience, is a simple
but consistent and predictable method for contouring and augmenting the soft tissue. I frequently use this technique to refine and correct the
contour deficiency.
The deficiency should be overcorrected
20–30%. The overcorrection is apparent for several weeks or months.
Figure 10-53 The patient was seen initially at age 6
years with localized scleroderma causing hemifacial atrophy. Surgical correction included orthognathic
surgery and dermal fat graft cheek augmentation.
Figure 10-54 The size and thickness of the dermal fat
graft is determined by the size and volume of the defect.
An overcorrection of 20–30% is recommended. An ellipse of deepithelialized skin and fat are marked for excision from the groin or buttocks. The donor site is
closed. The amount of fat left on the dermal graft depends on the size and volume of the defect.
Figure 10-55
insertion.

The deepithelialized graft is ready for

Figure 10-56 After orthognathic maxillary and
mandibular osteotomies with onlay bone graft by using
techniques demonstrated earlier in the Atlas, the final
correction of the contour of soft tissue is done with a
dermal fat graft. The pocket of undermined skin is
slightly larger than the contour defect. The graft is sutured at points around the edge of the defect to stabilize
it in place.

Preoperative frontal view of a 6-year-old

patient with early onset of hemifacial atrophy due to

shows progression of the hemifacial atrophy after treatment with local pericranial and dermal fat grafting.

CHAPTER 10

RECONSTRUCTION OF MICROTIA BY USING 3-DIMENSIONAL
COSTAL CARTILAGE FRAMEWORK (3-D FRAME)
I

Satoru Nagata

The key to reconstruction of microtia is the fabrication of a good 3-D framework. My total surgical
focus today is ear reconstruction. I present here a
concise description of my current personal technique, which has resulted in consistent improved
results in microtia reconstruction over time. I continue to make refinements and changes. The key to
reconstruction of microtia is the fabrication of a
good 3-D framework. The second stage includes
good elevation of the ear after reconstruction. This
is achieved by using a temporoparietal fascia flap
posteriorly to allow good elevation of the ear after
reconstruction. Many personal techniques, modifications, and refinements allow me to achieve consistently excellent reconstruction of the ear in microtia.
Figure 10-66 The fabrication of the 3-D costal cartilage framework is performed in this series. The sixth

and seventh costal cartilages are harvested en bloc:
base frame (A), tragus unit (B), incisura intertragic
notch unit (C), eight costal cartilage (crus helicis–helical rim unit) (D), and ninth costal cartilage (antihelixsuperior and inferior crus unit) (E). The outline of the
base frame and the tragus unit to the base frame are
shown in this illustration.
Figure 10-67 The base frame is carved out. An attachment of the crus helicis–helical rim unit (D) to the posterior surface of the base frame (A) is shown. The superior and inferior crus terminate 3 mm from the helical
rim. A cut-down notch in the crus helicis–helical rim
unit is performed to match the base unit, strengthening
and stabilizing the 3-D frame.
Figure 10-68 Shown in this illustration is the fixation
of the tragus unit (B), incisura intertragic notch unit (C),
crus helicis–helical rim unit (D), and antihelix-superior
and inferior crus unit (E) to the base frame.

CHAPTER 10

I SECOND-STAGE OPERATIVE
PROCEDURE: EAR ELEVATION
I have designed the second-stage operative procedure to include the use of the temporoparietal fascia (TPF) flap used posteriorly to allow good elevation of the ear after reconstruction. Ear elevation
is improved with the use of an additional cartilage
graft and the TPF flap.
Figure 10-79 The outlines for the elevation of the
temporoparietal fascia (TPF) flap (A) and harvesting of
the ultradelicate split-thickness scalp skin (UDSTS; B)
for ear elevation.

Figure 10-80 Schematic illustration of the ear elevation. The TPF is elevated for covering the posterior aspect of the elevated auricle. Undermined skin of the
mastoid surface is advanced, and the excess skin is excised in a triangular fashion above the hairline to avoid
dog-ear formation. A costal-cartilage block, shaped to
that of the eminent concha, is prepared to be grafted for
each patient individually.
Figure 10-81 After grafting of the costal-cartilage
block, it is covered with the elevated TPF. The harvested UDSTS (B) is then used to cover the posterior
surface of the elevated auricle to the mastoid surface.
